On the path homology of Cayley digraphs and covering digraphs Thanks: The work is supported by Beijing Institute of Mathematical Sciences and Applications
Shaobo Di, Sergei O. Ivanov, Lev Mukoseev, Mengmeng Zhang

Abstract
. We develop a theory of covering digraphs, similar to the theory of covering spaces. By applying this theory to Cayley digraphs, we build a “bridge” between GLMY-theory and group homology theory, which helps to reduce path homology calculations to group homology computations. We show some cases where this approach allows us to fully express path homology in terms of group homology. To illustrate this method, we provide a path homology computation for the Cayley digraph of the additive group of rational numbers with a generating set consisting of inverses to factorials. The main tool in our work is a filtered simplicial set associated with a digraph, which we call the filtered nerve of a digraph.



Introduction

Grigoryan, Lin, Muranov, and Yau developed the theory of path homology for digraphs in an unpublished preprint
[
8
]
, which is now referred to as GLMY-theory or GLMY-homology theory
[
7
,
14
,
15
,
9
,
13
,
12
,
10
,
17
]
. This theory is in many respects parallel to the theory of homology of spaces. In particular, it is homotopy invariant in a sense appropriate for digraphs and there are Künneth formulas for box product and join. They also defined a notion of the fundamental group of a digraph, whose abelianization coincides with the first integral path homology group
[
7
]
. The notion of fundamental group was later extended to the notion of fundamental groupoid by Grigor’yan, Jimenez, and Muranov
[
11
]
.

In our work, we continue the analogy between algebraic topology for spaces and “algebraic topology for digraphs” by developing a theory of coverings for digraphs. To be more precise, we develop a theory of
l
-fundamental groups and
l
-coverings of digraphs, where
l\geq 1
is a natural parameter. For
l=1
this theory is just the ordinary theory of fundamental groups and covering spaces, where a digraph is treated as a one-dimensional space. The most important case for us is
l=2,
because in this case this theory is compatible with GLMY-theory, and the
2
-fundamental group
\pi_{1}^{2}(X)
coincides with the group considered in
[
7
,
11
]
. A digraph is called connected if its underlying undirected graph is connected. We prove that for the universal
2
-covering
U\to X
of a connected digraph
X
there is a spectral sequence

H_{*}(\pi^{2}_{1}(X),\mathsf{PH}_{*}(U))\Rightarrow\mathsf{PH}_{*}(X),

where
\mathsf{PH}_{*}(-)
denotes the path homology, and
H_{*}(\pi^{2}_{1}(X),-)
the group homology of the group
\pi_{1}^{2}(X)
. This spectral sequence is similar to the Serre spectral sequence of the homotopy fibration sequence
\mathpzc{U}\to\mathpzc{X}\to B\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{X})
for a space
\mathpzc{X}
and its universal cover
\mathpzc{U}.
This spectral sequence is especially useful when
\mathsf{PH}_{n}(U)=0
for
n\geq 1,
because in this case we have an equation

\mathsf{PH}_{*}(X)=H_{*}(\pi_{1}^{2}(X)),

which reduces the computation of the path homology to the homology of the fundamental group.

Next, we apply the obtained theory to the case of Cayley digraphs. We describe generating subsets
S
of a group
G
whose Cayley digraphs have a trivial
l
-fundamental group. We call them
l
-freely generating subsets.
Roughly speaking, these are subsets of groups such that all relations between its elements are determined by some relations of the form
s_{1}\dots s_{n}=t_{1}\dots t_{m}
for
n,m\leq l,s_{i},t_{j}\in S.
We show that the universal
2
-covering of the Cayley digraph
\mathsf{Cay}(G,S)
is a Cayley digraph of a
2
-freely generating subset
\tilde{S}
of a group
\tilde{G}
, which is obtained from
G
by “removing” all relations that are not of the form
s_{1}\dots s_{n}=t_{1}\dots t_{m}
for
0\leq n,m\leq 2
. Furthermore, we obtain that there is a short exact sequence

1\longrightarrow\pi^{2}_{1}(\mathsf{Cay}(G,S))\longrightarrow\tilde{G}\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow 1,

and, if we set
\rho:=\mathsf{Ker}(\tilde{G}\to G),
we obtain a spectral sequence

H_{*}(\rho,\mathsf{PH}_{*}(\mathsf{Cay}(\tilde{G},\tilde{S})))\Rightarrow\mathsf{PH}_{*}(\mathsf{Cay}(G,S)).

In a sense, this spectral sequence reduces the computation of path homology of Cayley digraphs to the computation of path homology of Cayley digraphs of
2
-freely generating subsets of groups and the computation of group homology with coefficients in certain modules. As well as in the general case, this spectral sequence is especially useful when
\mathsf{Cay}(\tilde{G},\tilde{S})
has trivial reduced path homology, because in this case we have an isomorphism

\mathsf{PH}_{*}(\mathsf{Cay}(G,S))\cong H_{*}(\rho).

Our experience with concrete calculations indicates that this happens quite often but not always. For further investigation of path homology of Cayley digraphs, it would be important to understand in which cases reduced path homology of a Cayley digraph of a
2
-freely generating subset of a group is trivial.

The most convenient class of groups for applying the theory we have developed is the class of abelian groups. It is well known that for a torsion free abelian group
\rho
its homology with coefficients in a commutative ring
\mathbb{K}
can be described as the exterior algebra
H_{*}(\rho)=\Lambda^{*}(\rho\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{K}).
Using this equality, we prove a general theorem, which allows us to compute the path homology of Cayley digraphs for many subsets of abelian groups (Theorem
4.11
).
For example, we compute path homology of the Cayley digraph of
\mathbb{Q}
with the generating subset consisting of the inverses of factorials:

\mathsf{PH}_{*}(\mathsf{Cay}(\mathbb{Q},\{1/n!\mid n\geq 2\}))\cong\Lambda^{*}(\mathbb{K}^{\oplus\mathbb{N}}).

Thereby, path homology modules of this Cayley digraph are infinitely generated free
\mathbb{K}
-modules in all positive degrees.

Before developing the theory of
l
-fundamental groups and
l
-coverings of digraphs, we needed to formulate the GLMY-theory (and magnitude homology theory) in the style of Asao
[
2
,
1
]
. Following Asao and a remark of Hepworth-Willerton
[
16
, Remark 46]
we construct a filtered space associated with a digraph
X

\mathscr{N}^{0}(X)\subseteq\mathscr{N}^{1}(X)\subseteq\mathscr{N}^{2}(X)\subseteq\dots\subseteq\mathscr{N}(X),

that we call the filtered nerve of the digraph. Here for each
l\geq 0
the simplicial set
\mathscr{N}^{l}(X)
is in some sense the minimal simplicial set generated by pathes of length
\leq l.
In particular, the dimension of
\mathscr{N}^{l}(X)
is at most
l.
Asao showed that the spectral sequence of this filtered space contains the information about both magnitude homology and path homology. Therefore, we refer to it as the magnitude-path spectral sequence. In this paper, we adhere to the ideology that the whole “homotopy digraph theory” should be interpreted in terms of the filtered space
\mathscr{N}^{*}(X)
. In particular, we define the
l
-fundamental group of a digraph as the fundamental group of
\mathscr{N}^{l}(X)
, and the
l
-coverings of the digraph correspond to the coverings of the space
\mathscr{N}^{l}(X).

1.
Filtered nerve of a digraph

1.1.
Paths and distance in a digraph

A digraph is a couple
X=(X_{0},A(X)),

where
X_{0}
is a set and
A(X)\subseteq(X_{0}\times X_{0})\setminus\Delta(X_{0})

and
\Delta(X_{0})=\{(x,x)\mid x\in X_{0}\}.
Elements of
X_{0}
are called vertices and elements of
A(X)
are called arrows (or directed edges, or arcs). We set

(1.1)

X_{1}:=A(X)\cup\Delta(X_{0}).

A morphism of digraphs
f:X\to Y
is a map
f:X_{0}\to Y_{0}
such that
(v,u)\in X_{1}
implies
(f(v),f(u))\in Y_{1}.
A disadvantage of the set of arrows
A(X)
is that it is not functorial by
X,
so we prefer to use
X_{1}.

An
n
-path in digraph
X
from a vertex
x
to a vertex
x^{\prime}
is a sequence of vertices
(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})
such that
(x_{i},x_{i+1})\in X_{1}
and
x=x_{0},x^{\prime}=x_{n}.
An
n
-path is called regular, if
x_{i}\neq x_{i+1}.
For
x,x^{\prime}\in X_{0}
we denote by
\mathsf{dist}(x,x^{\prime})
the minimal
n
such that there is an
n
-path from
x
to
x^{\prime}.
If such a path does not exists, we set
\mathsf{dist}(x,x^{\prime})=+\infty.
This gives a structure of extended quasi-metric space on
X_{0}
. In particular, for any vertex
x
we have
\mathsf{dist}(x,x)=0,
and for any three vertices
x,y,z
we have the triangle inequality

(1.2)

\mathsf{dist}(x,z)\leq\mathsf{dist}(x,y)+\mathsf{dist}(y,z).

Note that a map
f:X_{0}\to Y_{0}
is a morphism of digraphs
X\to Y
if and only if for any vertices
x,x^{\prime}
of
X
we have

(1.3)

\mathsf{dist}(f(x),f(x^{\prime}))\leq\mathsf{dist}(x,x^{\prime}).

We find it convenient to write the fact that a pair
(x,x^{\prime})
is an arrow in the form of the equality
\mathsf{dist}(x,x^{\prime})=1,
and the fact that there is a path from
x
to
x^{\prime}
in the form of the inequality
\mathsf{dist}(x,x^{\prime})<\infty.

1.2.
Accessible sequences and the nerve of a digraph

We say that a sequence
(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})
of vertices of
X
is
accessible
, if
\mathsf{dist}(x_{i},x_{i+1})<\infty
for each
0\leq i<n.
We consider a simplicial set
\mathscr{N}(X)
whose
n
-simplices are accessible sequences
(x_{0},\dots,x_{n}).
The face and degeneracy maps are defined by the formulas

(1.4)

\begin{split}d_{i}(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})&=(x_{0},\dots,\hat{x}_{i},\dots,x_{n}),\\
s_{i}(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})&=(x_{0},\dots,x_{i},x_{i},\dots,x_{n}).\end{split}

The simplicial set
\mathscr{N}(X)
will be called
nerve
of
X.
Degenerate simplices of this simplicial set are called non-regular accessible sequences, and non-degenerate simplices are called regular accessible sequences.

A preorder is a category whose hom-sets have cardinality not greater than
1.
For any digraph
X
we construct a preorder
\mathsf{Pre}(X)
whose objects are vertices of
X
and the hom-sets are described as follows

(1.5)

\mathsf{Pre}(X)(x,y)=\begin{cases}\{(x,y)\},&\mathsf{dist}(x,y)<\infty\\
\emptyset,&\text{ else.}\end{cases}

Proposition 1.1
.

The nerve of
X
is naturally isomorphic to the nerve of the preorder

\mathscr{N}(X)\cong{\sf Nrv}(\mathsf{Pre}(X)).

Proof.

The correspondence
(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})\mapsto((x_{0},x_{1}),(x_{1},x_{2}),\dots,(x_{n-1},x_{n}))
defines the isomorphism.
∎

1.3.
Filtration of the nerve

Following Asao
[
2
, Def.2.3]
we define the length of an accessible sequence by the formula

(1.6)

{\sf L}((x_{0},\dots,x_{n})):=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\mathsf{dist}(x_{i},x_{i+1}).

Using the triangle inequality (
1.2
), it is easy to check that the following properties are satisfied

(1.7)

{\sf L}(d_{i}(\sigma))\leq{\sf L}(\sigma),\hskip 28.45274pt{\sf L}(s_{i}(\sigma))=(\sigma)

for any
\sigma\in\mathscr{N}(X)_{n}
and any
0\leq i\leq n.

For any natural
l
we denote by

\mathscr{N}^{l}(X)_{n}
the subset of
\mathscr{N}(X)_{n}
consisting of accessible sequences
\sigma
such that
{\sf L}(\sigma)\leq l.
The inequalities (
1.7
) show that these subsets define a simplicial subset
\mathscr{N}^{l}(X)\subseteq\mathscr{N}(X).
For all
l=0,1,\dots
it gives an exhaustive filtration of
\mathscr{N}(X)

(1.8)

\mathscr{N}^{0}(X)\subseteq\mathscr{N}^{1}(X)\subseteq\mathscr{N}^{2}(X)\subseteq\dots\subseteq\mathscr{N}(X),\hskip 14.22636pt\mathscr{N}(X)=\bigcup_{l=0}^{\infty}\mathscr{N}^{l}(X).

We also consider the quotients

(1.9)

\mathscr{M}^{l}(X):=\mathscr{N}^{l}(X)/\mathscr{N}^{l-1}(X)

for
l\geq 1
and
\mathscr{M}^{0}(X):=\mathscr{N}^{0}(X)\sqcup*.
The simplicial set
\mathscr{M}^{l}(X)
will be called
l
-th magnitude space of
X
.

It is easy to see that any accessible sequence
(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})
of length
<n
is non-regular. Therefore all simplices of
\mathscr{N}^{l}(X)_{n}
are degenerate for
n>l.
It follows that

(1.10)

{\sf dim}(\mathscr{N}^{l}(X))\leq l.

It is easy to see that
\mathscr{N}^{l}(X)
is the minimal simplicial subset of
\mathscr{N}(X)
containing all regular paths of length
\leq l.

1.4.
Filtered nerve of the box product

The box product
X\square Y
of digraphs
X
and
Y
is a digraph such that
(X\square Y)_{0}=X_{0}\times Y_{0}
and
((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))\in(X\square Y)_{1}
if and only if
((x,x^{\prime}),(y,y^{\prime}))\in(X_{1}\times\Delta(Y_{0}))\cup(\Delta(X_{0})\times Y_{1})

(1.11)

(X\square Y)_{1}\cong(X_{1}\times\Delta(Y_{0}))\cup(\Delta(X_{0})\times Y_{1}).

Lemma 1.2
.

A sequence of couples of vertices

((x_{0},y_{0}),\dots,(x_{n},y_{n}))
is a path in
X\square Y
if and only if
(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})
and
(y_{0},\dots,y_{n})
are paths in
X
and
Y
and there exists a partition
I\sqcup J=\{1,\dots,n\}
such that
x_{i-1}=x_{i}
and
y_{j-1}=y_{j}
for any
i\in I,j\in J.

Proof.

Obvious.
∎

Corollary 1.3
.

The distance in the box product satisfy the following equation

(1.12)

\mathsf{dist}_{X\Box Y}((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))=\mathsf{dist}_{X}(x,x^{\prime})+\mathsf{dist}_{Y}(y,y^{\prime}).

Proposition 1.4

(cf.
[
16
, Prop.41.]
)
.

For any digraphs
X,Y
there is an natural isomorphism

\mathscr{N}(X\square Y)\cong\mathscr{N}(X)\times\mathscr{N}(Y),

and for any
l
this isomorphism induces a isomorphisms

\mathscr{N}^{l}(X\square Y)\cong\bigcup_{l_{1}+l_{2}=l}\mathscr{N}^{l_{1}}(X)\times\mathscr{N}^{l_{2}}(Y),\hskip 14.22636pt\mathscr{M}^{l}(X\square Y)\cong\bigvee_{l_{1}+l_{2}=l}\mathscr{M}^{l_{1}}(X)\wedge\mathscr{M}^{l_{2}}(Y)

Proof.

We define the map
f:\mathscr{N}(X\square Y)\to\mathscr{N}(X)\times\mathscr{N}(Y)
by the formula

((x_{0},y_{0}),\dots,(x_{n},y_{n}))\mapsto((x_{0},\dots,x_{n}),(y_{0},\dots,y_{n})).

Corollary
1.3
implies that

(1.13)

{\sf L}((x_{0},y_{0}),\dots,(x_{n},y_{n}))={\sf L}(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})+{\sf L}(y_{0},\dots,y_{n}).

The first two isomorphisms follow. For simplicity we set
Z^{l}:=\mathscr{N}^{l}(Z).
Then the last isomorphism follows from the formulas

(X^{l_{1}}\times Y^{l_{2}})\cap(X^{l_{1}^{\prime}}\times Y^{l_{2}^{\prime}})=X^{{\sf min}(l_{1},l_{1}^{\prime})}\times Y^{{\sf min}(l_{2},l^{\prime}_{2})}\subseteq f((X\square Y)^{l-1})

if
(l_{1},l_{2})\neq(l_{1}^{\prime},l_{2}^{\prime})
and
l_{1}+l_{2}=l=l^{\prime}_{1}+l^{\prime}_{2},
and

(X^{l_{1}}\times Y^{l_{2}})\cap f((X\square Y)^{l-1})=(X^{l_{1}-1}\times Y^{l_{2}})\cup(X^{l_{1}}\times Y^{l_{2}-1}).

∎

1.5.
Reminder on homology of simplicial sets

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a commutative ring. For a simplicial
\mathbb{K}
-module
M
we denote by
C(M)
the chain complex with components
C(M)_{n}=M_{n}
and the differential
\partial_{n}:C(M)_{n}\to C(M)_{n-1}
defined by
\partial_{n}=\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}d_{i}.
We denote by
D(M)
the subcomplex of degenerate simplices of
C(M)
whose components are
D(M)_{n}=\sum_{i}s_{i}(M_{n-1}).
The
Moore complex
(or normalised complex) of
M
is the quotient complex

N(M)=C(M)/D(M).

It is well known that
N
is an exact functor.
It is also well known that
D(M)
is contractible and

H_{*}(N(M))\cong H_{*}(C(M)).

For a simplicial set
S
we denote by
\mathbb{K}(S)
the free simplicial
\mathbb{K}
-module whose module of
n
-simplices
\mathbb{K}(S)_{n}=\mathbb{K}(S_{n})
is freely generated by
S_{n}.
Then the normalised complex of
S
and homology of
S
are defined by

(1.14)

N(S)=N(\mathbb{K}(S)),\hskip 28.45274ptH_{*}(S)=H_{*}(N(S)),

we will also use notation
C(S)=C(\mathbb{K}(S))
and
D(S)=D(\mathbb{K}(S)).

If
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
is a simplicial subset, then
\mathbb{K}(S^{\prime})
is a simplicial submodule of
\mathbb{K}(S)
, and, since
N
is an exact functor,
N(S^{\prime})\to N(S)
is a monomorphism. We will usually identify it with its image
N(S^{\prime})\subseteq N(S).

If
S=(S,*)
is a pointed simplicial set, the reduced versions of the normalized complex of
S
and homology are defined by the formulas

(1.15)

\tilde{N}(S)=N(S)/N(*),\hskip 28.45274pt\tilde{H}_{*}(S):=H_{*}(\tilde{N}(S)).

For any simplicial subset
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
we define
S/S^{\prime}
as the pushout of the diagram
*\leftarrow S^{\prime}\to S.
If
S^{\prime}
is non-empty, then
(S/S^{\prime})_{n}
is just the quotient of
S_{n}
by the equivalence relation, where all elements of
S^{\prime}_{n}
are identified. If
S^{\prime}=\emptyset,
then
S/\emptyset=S\sqcup*.
Anyway
S/S^{\prime}
has a natural base-point and

(1.16)

\tilde{N}(S/S^{\prime})\cong N(S)/N(S^{\prime}),\hskip 28.45274pt\tilde{H}_{*}(S/S^{\prime})\cong H_{*}(N(S)/N(S^{\prime})).

If
S^{\prime}
is a pointed simplicial subset of
S
we also have a formula
\tilde{N}(S/S^{\prime})\cong\tilde{N}(S)/\tilde{N}(S^{\prime}),
which implies that there is a long exact sequence

(1.17)

\dots\to\tilde{H}_{n}(S^{\prime})\to\tilde{H}_{n}(S)\to\tilde{H}_{n}(S/S^{\prime})\overset{\delta}{\to}\tilde{H}_{n-1}(S^{\prime})\to\dots.

The homomorphism
\delta
is called connecting homomorphism for the cofiber sequence
S^{\prime}\hookrightarrow S\to S/S^{\prime}
.

1.6.
Chain complex of regular accessible sequences

Let
\mathbb{K}
be a commutative ring. For a digraph
X
we denote by
\Lambda(X)
a chain complex such that

\Lambda_{n}(X)
is an
\mathbb{K}
-module
spanned by
accessible
sequences
(x_{0},\dots,x_{n}).
The differential

\partial_{n}:\Lambda_{n}(X)\to\Lambda_{n-1}(X)

is defined by the formula

(1.18)

\partial_{n}(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}(x_{0},\dots,\hat{x_{i}},\dots,x_{n}).

We denote by
D(X)
a chain subcomplex of
\Lambda(X)
generated by non-regular sequences. Finally we set

(1.19)

\mathcal{R}(X)=\Lambda(X)/D(X).

For an element
a\in\Lambda_{n}(X)
we denote by
[a]
its image in
\mathcal{R}_{n}(X),
and for an accessible sequence of vertices
(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})
we set
[x_{0},\dots,x_{n}]:=[(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})].

Proposition 1.5
.

For a digraph
X
we have

(1.20)

\Lambda(X)=C(\mathscr{N}(X)),\hskip 28.45274pt\mathcal{R}(X)=N(\mathscr{N}(X)).

Moreover, an accessible sequence of vertices
(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})
is non-regular if and only if it is in
D(\mathscr{N}(X)).

Proof.

Obvious.
∎

1.7.
Magnitude homology

For a digraph
X
we denote by
\mathsf{MC}^{\leq l}(X)
a chain subcomplex of
\mathcal{R}(X)
generated by the images of accessible sequences
[x_{0},\dots,x_{n}]
with
{\sf L}(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})\leq l.
We also assume that
\mathsf{MC}^{\leq-1}(X)=0.
We define the magnitude complex and magnitude homology by the formulas

(1.21)

\mathsf{MC}^{l}(X)=\mathsf{MC}^{\leq l}(X)/\mathsf{MC}^{\leq l-1}(X),\hskip 28.45274pt\mathsf{MH}^{l}_{n}(X)=H_{n}(\mathsf{MC}^{l}(X)).

We call
\mathsf{MC}^{l}(X)
the
l
-th magnitude chain complex of
X.
For an accessible sequence
\sigma=(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})
such that
{\sf L}(\sigma)\leq l
we denote by
\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket
its image in
\mathsf{MC}^{l}(X).
Then

(1.22)

\{\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket\mid{\sf L}(\sigma)=l,\sigma\text{ is regular}\}

is a basis of
\mathsf{MC}^{l}(X).

Since any regular sequence
(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})
has length at least
n,
we obtain

(1.23)

\mathsf{MC}^{\leq l}_{n}(X)=\mathsf{MC}^{l}_{n}(X)=\mathsf{MH}^{l}_{n}(X)=0\text{ for }n>l.

We also obtain that

(1.24)

\mathsf{MH}^{n}_{n}(X)={\sf Ker}(\partial:\mathsf{MC}^{n}_{n}(X)\to\mathsf{MC}^{n}_{n-1}(X)).

Proposition 1.6
.

Our definition of
\mathsf{MC}^{l}(X)
(up to a natural isomorphism) coincides with the definition given by Asao
[
2
, Def.2.13]
.

Proof.

Since the digraph
X
is fixed, we will omit
X
in the notation in the proof
\mathsf{MC}^{l}:=\mathsf{MC}^{l}(X),\mathcal{R}_{n}:=\mathcal{R}_{n}(X)
and so on. Denote the complex defined by Asao by
\overline{\mathsf{MC}}^{l}.

Asao defines
\overline{\mathsf{MC}}^{l}_{n}
as an
\mathbb{K}
-submodule of
\mathcal{R}_{n}
spanned by regular sequences
\sigma
such that
{\sf L}(\sigma)=l.
Then

(1.25)

\{[\sigma]\mid{\sf L}(\sigma)=l,\sigma\text{ is regular}\}

is a basis of
\overline{\mathsf{MC}}^{l}.

Note that the assumption
{\sf L}(\sigma)\leq l
implies that the sequence
\sigma
is accessible. Asao uses a map
(-)^{l}:\mathcal{R}_{n}\to\mathcal{R}_{n}
such that for any accessible sequence
\sigma
we have
\sigma^{l}=\sigma,
if
{\sf L}(\sigma)=l,
and
\sigma^{l}=0,
if
{\sf L}(\sigma)\neq l.

He defines the differential
\bar{\partial}_{n}:\overline{\mathsf{MC}}^{l}_{n}\to\overline{\mathsf{MC}}^{l}_{n-1}
by the formula
\bar{\partial}(\sigma)=\partial(\sigma)^{l},
where
\partial
is the differential of
R.

Consider the map

\theta_{n}^{l}:\mathsf{MC}^{\leq l}_{n}\to\overline{\mathsf{MC}}^{l}_{n}

defined as the restriction of
(-)^{l}.
Note that
\mathsf{Ker}(\theta_{n}^{l})=\mathsf{MC}^{\leq l-1}_{n}.
We claim that
\theta^{l}=(\theta^{l}_{n})_{n}
is a morphism of complexes. Indeed, for any
a\in\mathsf{MC}^{\leq l}_{n}
we have
a-a^{l}\in\mathsf{MC}^{\leq l-1}_{n},
and hence,
\theta^{l}_{n}(a)=\theta^{l}_{n}(a^{l}).
Therefore
\bar{\partial}_{n}\theta_{n}^{l}(a)=\partial(a^{l})^{l}=\partial(a)^{l}=\theta_{n-1}^{l}\partial(a).
It follows that
\theta^{l}:\mathsf{MC}^{\leq l}\to\overline{\mathsf{MC}}^{l}
is a morphism of complexes. Since
\mathsf{Ker}(\theta^{l})=\mathsf{MC}^{\leq l-1},
we obtain that
\theta^{l}
induces a morphism

\tilde{\theta}^{l}:\mathsf{MC}^{l}\to\overline{\mathsf{MC}}^{l},
which induces a bijection on the bases (
1.25
), (
1.22
). Therefore
\tilde{\theta}^{l}
is an isomorphism of complexes.
∎

Lemma 1.7

(cf.
[
16
, Lemma 42]
)
.

There are natural isomorphisms

(1.26)

\mathsf{MC}^{\leq l}(X)\cong N(\mathscr{N}^{l}(X)),\hskip 28.45274pt\mathsf{MC}^{l}(X)\cong\tilde{N}(\mathscr{M}^{l}(X)).

Proof.

The embedding
\mathscr{N}^{l}(X)\hookrightarrow\mathscr{N}(X)
induces an embedding
N(\mathscr{N}^{l}(X))\hookrightarrow N(\mathscr{N}(X))=\Lambda(X),
whose image is obviously
\mathsf{MC}^{\leq l}(X).
The first isomorphism follows. The second isomorphism follows from (
1.16
).
∎

Theorem 1.8

(cf.
[
16
, Remark 45]
)
.

The magnitude homology can be described as reduced homology of the magnitude space

(1.27)

\mathsf{MH}^{l}_{n}(X)=\tilde{H}_{n}(\mathscr{M}^{l}(X)).

Proof.

It follows from
Lemma
1.7
and Equation (
1.16
).
∎

1.8.
Path homology and GLMY-theory

Denote by
\mathcal{A}_{n}(X)
a submodule of
\mathcal{R}_{n}(X)
generated by elements
[x_{0},\dots,x_{n}],
where
(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})
is a path in
X.
Then we denote by
\Omega(X)
a maximal chain subcomplex of
\mathcal{R}(X)
such that its
n
-th component
\Omega_{n}(X)
is a submodule in
\mathcal{A}_{n}(X).
Its components can be defined by the formula

\Omega_{n}(X)=\partial^{-1}(\mathcal{A}_{n-1}(X))\cap\mathcal{A}_{n}(X).
Its homology is called path homology of
X
or GLMY-homology of
X

(1.28)

\mathsf{PH}_{*}(X)=H_{*}(\Omega(X)).

Proposition 1.9
.

The chain complex
\Omega(X)
is naturally isomorphic to the chain complex
\Omega^{\sf reg}(X)
defined by Grigonyan, Lin, Muranov, Yau in
[
8
]
.

Proof.

The definition of Grigonyan, Lin, Muranov, Yau differs from our in that they consider all sequences of vertices while we consider only accessible sequences.
They define a complex that we denote by
\Lambda^{\prime}(X),
whose components
\Lambda^{\prime}(X)_{n}
are freely generated by all sequences of vertices
(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})
with the same formula for the differential (
1.18
). This complex also has a structure of a simplicial module and they consider the quotient
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(X)=N(\Lambda^{\prime}(X)).
The embedding
\Lambda(X)\subseteq\Lambda^{\prime}(X)
induces an embedding
\iota:\mathcal{R}(X)\rightarrowtail\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(X)
. They consider submodules
A^{\prime}_{n}(X)\subseteq\mathcal{R}^{\prime}(X)_{n}
generated by the images of paths of
X,
and define
\Omega^{\sf reg}(X)
as the maximal subcomplex of
R^{\prime}(X)
such that
\Omega^{\sf reg}_{n}(X)\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{n}(X).
It is easy to see that
\iota(\mathcal{A}_{n}(X))=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{n}(X).
Then the assertion follows from
[
17
, Remark 3.1]
.
∎

Remark 1.10
.

We prefer to use the term “path” in the ordinary combinatorial meaning. We use the term “GLMY-chains” or “path chains” for homogeneous elements of
\Omega(X),
and “GLMY-cycles” or “path cycles” for the cycles of this complex.

Asao proved that
\Omega_{n}(X)
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{MH}^{n}_{n}(X).
Here we provide a more homotopical version of Asao’s result.

Proposition 1.11

(sf.
[
2
, Lemma 6.8]
)
.

The chain complex
\Omega(X)
is isomorphic to the chain complex

(1.29)

\dots\overset{\delta_{3}}{\longrightarrow}\tilde{H}_{2}(\mathscr{M}^{2}(X))\overset{\delta_{2}}{\longrightarrow}\tilde{H}_{1}(\mathscr{M}^{1}(X))\overset{\delta_{1}}{\longrightarrow}\tilde{H}_{0}(\mathscr{M}^{0}(X)),

where
\delta_{n}:\tilde{H}_{n}(\mathscr{M}^{n}(X))\to\tilde{H}_{n-1}(\mathscr{M}^{n-1}(X))
is the connecting homomorphism for the cofiber sequence
\mathscr{M}^{n-1}(X)\hookrightarrow\mathscr{N}^{n}(X)/\mathscr{N}^{n-2}(X)\to\mathscr{M}^{n}(X).

Proof.

For simplicity in the proof we omit
X
in notations
\mathscr{M}^{n}:=\mathscr{M}^{n}(X)
,
\mathcal{A}_{n}:=\mathcal{A}_{n}(X)
….
Since
{\sf dim}(\mathscr{M}^{n})\leq n,
we have
\tilde{N}(\mathscr{M}^{n})_{n+1}=0
and
\tilde{N}(\mathscr{M}^{n})_{n}\cong N(\mathscr{N}^{n})_{n}=\mathcal{A}_{n}.
Therefore,

(1.30)

\tilde{H}_{n}(\mathscr{M}^{n})=\mathsf{Ker}(\mathcal{A}_{n}\to N(\mathscr{N}^{n})_{n-1}/\mathcal{A}_{n-1}).

It follows that
\tilde{H}_{n}(\mathscr{M}^{n})\cong\partial^{-1}(\mathcal{A}_{n-1})\cap\mathcal{A}_{n}=\Omega_{n}.
The map
\delta_{n}
is the connecting homomorphism for the short exact sequence of complexes

(1.31)

0\to\tilde{N}(\mathscr{M}^{n-1})\to\tilde{N}(\mathscr{N}^{n}/\mathscr{N}^{n-2})\to\tilde{N}(\mathscr{M}^{n})\to 0,

which is induced by the differential of
\tilde{N}(\mathscr{N}^{n}/\mathscr{N}^{n-2}).
The differential on
\tilde{N}(\mathscr{N}^{n}/\mathscr{N}^{n-2})

=N(\mathscr{N}^{n})/N(\mathscr{N}^{n-2})
coincides with the differential induced by the differential on
\Lambda.
So
\delta_{n}
is induced by the differential on
\Lambda
and the differential of
\Omega
is also induced by the differential on
\Lambda.
Using this one can check that
\Omega
is isomorphic to the complex (
1.29
).
∎

The chain complex
\mathcal{R}(X)
has a structure of a semi-simplicial module defined by the maps

(1.32)

d_{i}:\mathcal{R}(X)_{n}\longrightarrow\mathcal{R}(X)_{n-1},\hskip 14.22636pt[x_{0},\dots,x_{n}]\mapsto[x_{0},\dots,\hat{x}_{i},\dots,x_{n}].

This structure can be used to give an alternative description of
\Omega(X)_{n}.

Proposition 1.12
.

The following holds

\Omega_{n}(X)=\{a\in\mathcal{A}_{n}(X)\mid d_{i}(a)\in\mathcal{A}_{n-1}(X)\text{ for any }1\leq i\leq n-1\}.

Proof.

For simplicity, in the proof we will omit
X
in the notation:
R=\mathcal{R}(X),\Lambda=\Lambda(X),D=D(X)
and so on.
For a regular accessible sequence
(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})
we define its type as the sequence of the distances

(\mathsf{dist}(x_{0},x_{1}),\mathsf{dist}(x_{1},x_{2}),\dots,\mathsf{dist}(x_{n-1},x_{n})).
For a sequence of positive natural numbers
(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})
we denote by
\mathcal{R}_{(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})}
the submodule of
\mathcal{R}_{n}
freely generated by accessible sequences of type
(k_{1},\dots,k_{n}).
Then

(1.33)

\mathcal{R}_{n}=\bigoplus_{(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})\in\mathbf{N}_{>0}^{n}}\mathcal{R}_{(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})},

and

\mathcal{A}_{n}=\mathcal{R}_{\mathbf{1}},
where
\mathbf{1}=(1,\dots,1).
For any element
r\in\mathcal{R}_{n}
we denote by
r_{(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})}
its component in
\mathcal{R}_{(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})}.
Then
r\in A_{n}
if and only if
r=r_{\mathbf{1}}.

For
1\leq i\leq n-1
we denote by
t_{i}
the vector
(1,\dots,1,2,1,\dots,1)\in\mathbb{N}^{n-1}_{>0}
where
2
is on the
i
-th position. Then we have
d_{i}(\mathcal{A}_{n})\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{n-1}\oplus\mathcal{R}_{t_{i}}
and
d_{0}(\mathcal{A}_{n}),d_{n}(\mathcal{A}_{n})\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{n-1}.
Hence, for any
a\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
we obtain
d_{i}(a)=d_{i}(a)_{\mathbf{1}}+d_{i}(a)_{t_{i}}.
Therefore, for any
a\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
we get

(1.34)

\begin{split}\partial_{n}(a)_{\mathbf{1}}&=d_{0}(a)+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{i}d_{i}(a)_{\mathbf{1}}+(-1)^{n}d_{n}(a),\\
\partial_{n}(a)_{t_{i}}&=d_{i}(a)_{t_{i}},\hskip 28.45274pt1\leq i\leq n-1,\end{split}

and
\partial_{n}(a)_{(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})}=0
for all
(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})\notin\{\mathbf{1},t_{1},\dots,t_{n-1}\}.

Therefore
\partial_{n}(a)\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
if and only if
d_{i}(a)_{t_{i}}=0
for any
1\leq i\leq n.
In other words,
\partial_{n}(a)\in\mathcal{A}_{n}
if and only if
d_{i}(a)=d_{i}(a)_{\mathbf{1}}
for any
1\leq i\leq n.
The assertion follows.
∎

1.9.
Spectral sequence of a filtered simplicial set

For a chain complex
C
with an exhaustive filtration

(1.35)

0=F_{-1}C\subseteq F_{0}C\subseteq F_{1}C\subseteq\dots\subseteq C,\hskip 28.45274ptC=\bigcup_{i}F_{i}C

there is a natural spectral sequence
E
converging to
H_{*}(C)
such that

(1.36)

E^{0}_{i,j}=F_{j}C_{i+j}/F_{j-1}C_{i+j},\hskip 14.22636ptE^{1}_{i,j}=H_{i+j}(F_{j}C/F_{j-1}C)

and

(1.37)

E^{r}_{i,j}={\sf Im}(H_{i+j}(F_{i}C/F_{i-r}C)\longrightarrow H_{i+j}(F_{i+r-1}C/F_{i-1}C)).

for
r\geq 1

(see
[
20
, Th. 5.5]
,
[
5
, Ch.XV,§1,(8)]
,
[
18
, (4.13)]
). For a simplicial set
\mathpzc{X}
with an exhaustive filtration

(1.38)

\emptyset=\mathpzc{X}^{-1}\subseteq\mathpzc{X}^{0}\subseteq\mathpzc{X}^{1}\subseteq\dots\subseteq\mathpzc{X},\hskip 28.45274pt\mathpzc{X}=\bigcup_{i}\mathpzc{X}^{i}

we can consider the chain complex
C=N(\mathpzc{X})
with an exhaustive filtration defined by the images of the embeddings
F_{i}C={\sf Im}(N(\mathpzc{X}^{i})\rightarrowtail N(\mathpzc{X})).
Then the associated spectral sequence
E
satisfies the following

(1.39)

E^{\infty}_{i,j}\Rightarrow H_{i+j}(\mathpzc{X}),\hskip 14.22636ptE^{0}_{i,j}=\tilde{N}(\mathpzc{X}^{j}/\mathpzc{X}^{j-1})_{i+j},\hskip 14.22636ptE^{1}_{i,j}=\tilde{H}_{i+j}(\mathpzc{X}^{j}/\mathpzc{X}^{j-1})

and

(1.40)

E^{r}_{i,j}={\sf Im}(\tilde{H}_{i+j}(\mathpzc{X}^{i}/\mathpzc{X}^{i-r})\longrightarrow\tilde{H}_{i+j}(\mathpzc{X}^{i+r-1}/\mathpzc{X}^{i-1})).

for
r\geq 1.
This spectral sequence will be called the spectral sequence of the filtered simplicial set
\mathpzc{X}.
Note that it is natural by the filtered space
\mathpzc{X}.

1.10.
Magnitude-path spectral sequence

Here we present a new approach to the spectral sequence constructed by Asao in
[
2
, Prop. 7.8]
(see also
[
16
, Remark 46]
,
[
1
, Prop 4.34.]
). We construct it as the spectral sequence of a filtered simplicial set.

Proposition 1.13

(cf.
[
2
, Prop. 7.8]
,
[
16
, Remark 46]
,
[
1
, Prop 4.34.]
)
.

Let
X
be a digraph and

E(X)
be the spectral
sequence of the filtered nerve
\mathscr{N}^{*}(X).

Then

(1)

E^{r}_{i,j}(X)\Rightarrow H_{i+j}(\mathsf{Pre}(X));

(2)

E^{0}_{i,j}(X)=\mathsf{MC}^{j}_{i+j}(X),

E^{1}_{i,j}(X)=\mathsf{MH}^{j}_{i+j}(X);

(3)

E^{1}_{*,0}(X)\cong\Omega(X)
and
E^{2}_{n,0}(X)=\mathsf{PH}_{n}(X).

Proof.

It follows from the isomorphism
{\sf Nrv}({\sf Pre}(X))\cong\mathscr{N}(X)
(Proposition
1.1
), equality
\mathsf{MC}^{l}_{n}(X)=\tilde{H}_{n}(\mathscr{M}^{l}(X))

(Theorem
1.8
), and Proposition
1.11
.
∎

Proposition 1.14
.

The magnitude-path spectral sequence commutes with filtered colimits of digraphs

E^{r}_{*,*}({\sf colim}(X^{\alpha}))\cong{\sf colim}(E^{r}_{*,*}(X^{\alpha})).

In particular, magnitude and path homology commute with filtered colimits of digraphs.

Proof.

Let
X^{\alpha}
be a filtered diagram of digraphs (a functor from a filtered category) and
X={\sf colim}(X^{\alpha})
. We denote by
\iota^{\alpha}:X^{\alpha}\to X
the canonical maps. It is easy to see that
X_{0}={\sf colim}(X^{\alpha}_{0})
and
X_{1}={\sf colim}(X^{\alpha}_{1}).
Therefore, any path of length
n
in
X
can be lifted to a path of length
n
in
X^{\alpha}
for some
\alpha.
Hence
{\sf Pre}(X)\cong{\sf colim}\>{\sf Pre}(X^{\alpha}).

Any finite category is a compact object in a category of small categories. In particular, the poset
[n]=\{0<1<\dots<n\}
treated as a category is a compact object in the category of small
categories. Since
{\sf Nrv}_{n}(\mathpzc{C})={\sf Cat}([n],\mathpzc{C}),

we obtain that the functor of nerve
{\sf Nrv}:{\sf Cat}\to{\sf sSet}
commutes with filtered colimits. Hence
{\sf Pre}(X)\cong{\sf colim}\>{\sf Pre}(X^{\alpha})
implies
\mathscr{N}(X)\cong{\sf colim}\>\mathscr{N}(X^{\alpha}).

The simplicial set
{\sf colim}\>\mathscr{N}(X^{\alpha})
has a filtration defined by the images
{\sf colim}\>\mathscr{N}^{\ell}(X^{\alpha})\to{\sf colim}\>\mathscr{N}(X^{\alpha}).
Since any path of length
n
in
X
can be lifted to a path of length
n
in
X^{\alpha}
for some
\alpha,
we obtain that any simplex from
\mathscr{N}^{\ell}(X)
can be lifted to a simplex of
\mathscr{N}^{\ell}(X^{\alpha})
for some
\alpha.
Therefore we obtain an isomorphism of filtered simplicial sets
\mathscr{N}^{*}(X)\cong{\sf colim}\>\mathscr{N}^{*}(X^{\alpha}).
Using that filtered colimits commute with pushouts, we obtain

(1.41)

\mathscr{N}^{\ell}(X)/\mathscr{N}^{\ell^{\prime}}(X)\cong{\sf colim}\>\mathscr{N}^{\ell}(X^{\alpha})/\mathscr{N}^{\ell^{\prime}}(X^{\alpha})

for any
\ell\geq\ell^{\prime}.
Then the assertion follows from the formula (
1.40
) and the fact that the homology of a simplicial set commute with filtered colimits.
∎

Corollary 1.15
.

If
X
is a digraph and there is an increasing sequence of subdigraphs

X^{1}\subseteq X^{2}\subseteq\dots\subseteq X,
such that
X=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}X^{n},

then
\mathsf{PH}_{*}(X)={\sf colim}\>\mathsf{PH}_{*}(X^{n}).

2.
Fundamental groupoids of digraphs

2.1.
Category defined by a quiver with relations

When we work with small categories and groupoids, we prefer to write composition from the left to the right. In this case we use the dot

(2.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\cdot g=gf}

By a quiver we mean a
5
-tuple
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1},d_{0},d_{1},s_{0}),
where
Q_{0}
and
Q_{1}
are sets called “the set of vertices” and “the set of arrows”,
d_{0},d_{1}:Q_{1}\to Q_{0}
are maps called “head” and “tail” of an arrow, and
s_{0}:Q_{0}\to Q_{1}
is a map called “degeneracy map”, which satisfy

d_{0}s_{0}=d_{1}s_{0}={\sf id}_{Q_{0}}.

Arrows from
s_{0}(v)
are called degenerate loops, all other arrows are called non-degenerate arrows.
Since we think about quivers as about “categories without composition” we will also use the notation

(2.2)

1_{v}=s_{0}(v),\hskip 14.22636ptd_{0}(\alpha)={\sf cod}(\alpha),\hskip 14.22636ptd_{1}(\alpha)={\sf dom}(\alpha).

A quiver can be equivalently defined as a 1-truncated simplicial set. A morphism of quivers
f:Q\to Q^{\prime}
is a couple of maps
f_{0}:Q_{0}\to Q^{\prime}_{0}
and
f_{1}:Q_{1}\to Q^{\prime}_{1}
such that
d_{i}f_{1}=f_{0}d_{i}
and
s_{0}f_{0}=f_{1}s_{0}.

We define a path in a quiver
Q
from a vertex
v
to a vertex
u
is a sequence of arrows
(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})
such that
d_{1}(\alpha_{1})=v,d_{0}(\alpha_{i})=d_{1}(\alpha_{i+1}),d_{0}(\alpha_{n})=u.
We consider an equivalence relation on the set of paths, which is the minimal equivalence relation such that
(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})\ \sim\ (\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{i},1_{v_{i}},\alpha_{i+1},\dots,\alpha_{n}).

A class of paths containing
(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})

will be denoted by

\alpha_{1}\cdot{\dots}\cdot\alpha_{n}.

Any such a class can be rewritten either as
1_{v}
for some
v,
or as

\alpha_{1}\cdot{\dots}\cdot\alpha_{n},

where
\alpha_{i}
’s are non-degenerate arrows. The free category
F^{\sf cat}(Q)
is the category whose set of objects is
Q_{0}
and morphisms from
v
to
u
are equivalence classes of paths. The composition is defined by the concatenation, the identity morphism is defined as
1_{v}=s(v).
This category satisfies the obvious universal property. One can say that
F^{\sf cat}
is the left adjoint functor to the forgetful functor
U:{\sf Cat}\to{\sf Quiv}.

Assume that
\mathpzc{C}
is a small category and
\mathpzc{R}\subseteq{\sf Mor}(\mathpzc{C})^{2}
is a set of parallel pairs of morphisms i.e. for any
(r,r^{\prime})\in\mathpzc{R}
we have
{\sf dom}(r)={\sf dom}(r^{\prime})
and
{\sf cod}(r)={\sf cod}(r^{\prime})
. Then we denote by
\mathpzc{C}/\mathpzc{R}
the quotient category
\mathpzc{C}/\!\sim,
where
\sim
is the minimal congruence relation such that
r\sim r^{\prime}
for any
(r,r^{\prime})\in\mathpzc{R}.
The quotient category satisfies the following universal property. For any functor
\tilde{\Phi}:\mathpzc{C}\to\mathpzc{D}
such that
\tilde{\Phi}(r)=\tilde{\Psi}(r^{\prime})
for any
(r,r^{\prime})\in\mathpzc{R},
there exists a unique functor
\Phi:\mathpzc{C}/\mathpzc{R}\to\mathpzc{D}
such that
\Phi\pi=\tilde{\Phi},
where
\pi:\mathpzc{C}\to\mathpzc{C}/\mathpzc{R}
is the canonical projection.

If
\mathpzc{C}=F^{\sf cat}(Q),
the quotient category

(2.3)

\langle Q\mid\mathpzc{R}\rangle_{\sf cat}:=F^{\sf cat}(Q)/\mathpzc{R}.

is called the category defined by the quiver
Q
with relations
\mathpzc{R}.

2.2.
Groupoid defined by a quiver with relations

The free groupoid
F(Q)
generated by a quiver
Q
is defined as the localization of the free category by all morphisms

(2.4)

F(Q)=F^{\sf cat}(Q)[{\sf Mor}(F^{\sf cat}(Q))^{-1}].

It is easy to see that free groupoid satisfies the obvious universal property.

Equivalently the free groupoid can be defined as follows. Consider the quiver
Q^{\pm}
such that
Q^{\pm}_{0}=Q_{0}
and
Q^{\pm}_{1}
consists of elements of two types:
1_{v}
for
v\in Q_{0};
formal elements
\alpha^{\varepsilon},
where
\alpha\in Q_{1}\setminus s_{0}(Q_{0})
and
\varepsilon\in\{-1,1\}.
The maps
d_{0},d_{1},s_{0}
are defined by
s_{0}(v)=1_{v},

d_{k}(1_{v})=v,

d_{k}(\alpha^{1})=d_{k}(\alpha)
and
d_{k}(\alpha^{-1})=d_{1-k}(\alpha).
Then it is easy to see that

(2.5)

F(Q)\cong\langle Q^{\pm}\mid\alpha^{\varepsilon}\cdot\alpha^{-\varepsilon}=1_{d_{0}(\alpha^{\varepsilon})}\rangle_{\sf cat}.

If
X
is a digraph, we can consider the associated quiver
Q(X),
where
Q(X)_{0}=X_{0},

Q(X)_{1}=X_{1},

s_{0}(x)=(x,x),

d_{0}(x_{0},x_{1})=x_{1},

d_{1}(x_{0},x_{1})=x_{0}
and set
F(X)=F(Q(X)).

If
\mathpzc{R}\subseteq{\sf Mor}(F(Q))^{2}
is a set of parallel pairs of morphisms, we set

\langle Q\mid\mathpzc{R}\rangle=F(Q)/\mathpzc{R}.

The groupoid
\langle Q\mid\mathpzc{R}\rangle
is called the groupoid defined by the quiver
Q
with relations
\mathpzc{R}.
If
X
is a digraph, we also set

(2.6)

\langle X\mid\mathpzc{R}\rangle=F(X)/\mathpzc{R},

and call this groupoid a groupoid defined by a digraph with relations.

2.3.
Fundamental groupoid of a simplicial set

For a simplicial set
\mathpzc{X}
the fundamental groupoid
\Pi(\mathpzc{X})
is the groupoid defined by the quiver

(2.7)

Q(\mathpzc{X})=(\mathpzc{X}_{0},\mathpzc{X}_{1},d_{0},d_{1},s_{0})

and relations

(2.8)

d_{2}(z)\cdot d_{0}(z)=d_{1}(z)

for any
z\in\mathpzc{X}_{2}.
Note that by the definition of a free groupoid we also have

s_{0}(x)=1_{x}
for any
x\in\mathpzc{X}_{0}.

2.4.
l
-fundamental groupoid of a digraph

In this subsection we will prove that for a digraph
X
the fundamental groupoid of
\mathscr{N}^{l}(X)
is the groupoid defined by the digraph
X
, where for any two vertices
x,x^{\prime}
all paths from
x
to
x^{\prime}
of lengths
\leq l
are identified. We will call
\Pi(\mathscr{N}^{l}(X))
the
l
-fundamental groupoid of
X
and denote it by

\Pi^{l}(X):=\Pi(\mathscr{N}^{l}(X)).

Lemma 2.1
.

For any path
(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})
in
X
such that
0\leq n\leq l
we have the following equation in
\Pi^{l}(X)

(2.9)

(x_{0},x_{1})\cdot(x_{1},x_{2})\cdot{\dots}\cdot(x_{n-1},x_{n})=(x_{0},x_{n}).

Proof.

This can be proved by induction on
n.
For
n=0,1
it is obvious. The inductive step follows from the computation

(2.10)

\begin{split}(x_{0},x_{1})\cdot(x_{1},x_{2})\cdot{\dots}\cdot(x_{n-1},x_{n})&=(x_{0},x_{n-1})\cdot(x_{n-1},x_{n})\\
&=d_{2}(x_{0},x_{n-1},x_{n})\cdot d_{0}(x_{0},x_{n-1},x_{n})\\
&=d_{1}(x_{0},x_{n-1},x_{n})=(x_{0},x_{n}).\end{split}

∎

Proposition 2.2
.

For any digraph
X

the
l
-fundamental groupoid
\Pi^{l}(X)
is a groupoid naturally isomorphic to a groupoid defined by the digraph
X
with relations

(2.11)

(x_{0},x_{1})\cdot(x_{1},x_{2})\cdot{\dots}\cdot(x_{n-1},x_{n})=(y_{0},y_{1})\cdot(y_{1},y_{2})\cdot{\dots}\cdot(y_{m-1},y_{m})

where
x_{0}=y_{0},

x_{n}=y_{m},

(x_{i},x_{i+1}),(y_{j},y_{j+1})\in X_{1}
and
0\leq n,m\leq l.

Proof.

For the proof we denote by
\mathpzc{G}
the groupoid defined by
X
with relations (
2.11
) and
\Pi^{l}:=\Pi^{l}(X)
. We need to prove that
\mathpzc{G}\cong\Pi^{l}.
The plan of the proof is the following. We will construct two functors
\Phi:\mathpzc{G}\to\Pi^{l}
and
\Psi:\Pi^{l}\to\mathpzc{G}
and prove that
\Psi\Phi={\sf Id}
and
\Phi\Psi={\sf Id}.
In order to construct the functors
\Phi
and
\Psi,
we first construct functors
\tilde{\Phi}:F(X)\to\Pi^{l}
and
\tilde{\Psi}:F(Q(\mathscr{N}^{l}(X)))\to\mathpzc{G}.

Since
Q(X)\subseteq Q(\mathscr{N}^{l}(X)),
we
can consider a functor
\tilde{\Phi}:F(Q(X))\to\Pi^{l}
which is identical on vertices and arrows in
Q(X).
Lemma
2.1
implies that the relations (
2.11
) hold in
\Pi^{l}
.
Therefore,
\tilde{\Psi}
induces a functor
\Phi:\mathpzc{G}\to\Pi^{l}
.

The set
Q(\mathscr{N}^{l}(X))_{1}
consists of couples
(x,x^{\prime})
such that
\mathsf{dist}(x,x^{\prime})\leq l.

We define a functor
\tilde{\Psi}:F(Q(\mathscr{N}^{l}(X)))\to\mathpzc{G}
on the quiver
Q(\mathscr{N}^{l}(X))
so that it is identical on vertices and sends an arrow
(x,x^{\prime})
to a path from
x
to
x^{\prime}
of length
\leq l:

(2.12)

\tilde{\Psi}((x,x^{\prime}))=(y_{0},y_{1})\cdot(y_{1},y_{2})\cdot{\dots}\cdot(y_{n-1},y_{n}),

where
y_{0}=x,y_{n}=x^{\prime},\mathsf{dist}(y_{i},y_{i+1})\leq 1
and
0\leq n\leq l.
Relations (
2.11
) show that the definition does not depend on the choice of the path. In order to prove that
\tilde{\Psi}
induces a functor
\Psi:\Pi^{l}\to\mathpzc{G},
we need to check that

(2.13)

\tilde{\Psi}(d_{2}((x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}))\cdot d_{0}((x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})))=\tilde{\Psi}(d_{1}((x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})))

for any
(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathscr{N}^{l}(X)_{2}.
We set
n:=\mathsf{dist}(x_{0},x_{1})
and
m:=\mathsf{dist}(x_{1},x_{2}).
Then
n+m\leq l.
Consider a path
(y_{0},\dots,y_{n})
from
x_{0}=y_{0}
to
x_{1}=y_{n}
, and a path
(y_{n},\dots,y_{n+m})
from
x_{1}=y_{n}
to
x_{2}=y_{n+m}.
Then

(2.14)

\begin{split}\tilde{\Psi}(d_{2}((x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}))\cdot d_{0}((x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})))&=\tilde{\Psi}((x_{0},x_{1}))\cdot\tilde{\Psi}((x_{1},x_{2}))\\
=((y_{0},y_{1})\cdot{\dots}\cdot(y_{n-1},y_{n}))&\cdot((y_{n},y_{n+1})\cdot{\dots}\cdot(y_{n+m-1},y_{n+m}))\\
&=(y_{0},y_{1})\cdot{\dots}\cdot(y_{n+m-1},y_{n+m})\\
&=\tilde{\Psi}((x_{0},x_{2}))=\tilde{\Psi}(d_{1}((x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}))).\end{split}

Therefore
\tilde{\Psi}
induces a functor
\Psi:\Pi^{l}\to\mathpzc{G}.

Let us check the identities
\Psi\Phi={\sf Id}_{\mathpzc{G}}
and
\Phi\Psi={\sf Id}_{\Pi^{l}}.
It is sufficient to check them only for arrows from the generating quivers. The identity
\Psi(\Phi((x,x^{\prime})))=(x,x^{\prime})
is obvious for any arrow
(x,x^{\prime})
. For any
(x,x^{\prime})\in Q(\mathscr{N}^{l}(X))_{1}
we take a path
(y_{0},\dots,y_{n})
such that
x=y_{0},x^{\prime}=y_{n},(x,x^{\prime})\in X_{1}
and
n\leq l.
Using Lemma
2.1
, we obtain

\Phi(\Psi(x,x^{\prime}))=(y_{0},y_{1})\cdot{\dots}\cdot(y_{n-1},y_{n})=(x,x^{\prime}).

∎

2.5.
Grigor’yan-Jimenez-Muranov groupoid

Grigor’yan,
Jimenez and Muranov in
[
11
]
define a version of the fundamental groupoid of a digraph that we denote by
\Pi^{\sf GJM}(X)
. They show that the corresponding version of the fundamental group coincides with the fundamental group defined by Grygor’yan, Lin, Muranov and Yau in
[
7
]
. In their definition they use the notion of a square and a triangle in a digraph. A
square
in
X
is a quadruple of distinct vertices
(x,y,y^{\prime},z)
such that
\mathsf{dist}(x,y)=\mathsf{dist}(x,y^{\prime})=\mathsf{dist}(y,z)=\mathsf{dist}(y^{\prime},z)=1.
A
triangle
in
X
is a triple of distinct vertices
(x,y,z)
such that
\mathsf{dist}(x,y)=\mathsf{dist}(y,z)=\mathsf{dist}(x,z)=1.
Their definition can be rewritten as follows. For a digraph
X
we consider a digraph
X^{\pm}
such that
X^{\pm}_{0}=X_{0}
and
(x,x^{\prime})\in X^{\pm}_{1}
if and only if
(x,x^{\prime})\in X_{1}
or
(x^{\prime},x)\in X_{1}.

Then the groupoid
\Pi^{\sf GJM}(X)
is the groupoid defined by the digraph
X^{\pm}
and relations

(GJM1’):

(x,y)\cdot(y,x)={\sf id}_{x};

(GJM2’):

(x,y)\cdot(y,z)=(x,z),
if
(\sigma(x),\sigma(y),\sigma(z))
is a triangle for some permutation
\sigma;

(GJM3’):

(x,y)\cdot(y,z)=(x,y^{\prime})\cdot(y^{\prime},z)
if
(\sigma(x),\sigma(y),\sigma(y^{\prime}),\sigma(z))
is a square, where
\sigma
is a power of the cyclic permutation
x\to y\to z\to y^{\prime}\to x.

(GJM4’):

(x,y)\cdot(y,z)\cdot(z,y^{\prime})=(x,y^{\prime})
if
(\sigma(x),\sigma(y),\sigma(y^{\prime}),\sigma(z))
is a square, where
\sigma
is a power of the cyclic permutation
x\to y\to z\to y^{\prime}\to x;

(GJM5’):

(x,x)=1_{x}.

Lemma 2.3
.

The relations
(GJM1’)-(GJM5’)
are equivalent to the following smaller set of relations:

(GJM1):

(x,y)\cdot(y,x)={\sf id}_{x};

(GJM2):

(x,y)\cdot(y,z)=(x,z),
if
(x,y,z)
is a triangle;

(GJM3):

(x,y)\cdot(y,z)=(x,y^{\prime})\cdot(y^{\prime},z),
if
(x,y,y^{\prime},z)
is a square.

Proof.

First note that our definition of the free groupoid we have
(x,x)=s(x)={\sf id}_{x}.
Hence we can skip
(GJM5’)
.
It is also easy to see that
(GJM1’)
and
(GJM3’)
imply
(GJM4’)

(2.15)

(x,y)\cdot(y,z)\cdot(z,y^{\prime})=(x,y^{\prime})\cdot(y^{\prime},z)\cdot(z,y^{\prime})=(x,y^{\prime})

if
(\sigma(x),\sigma(y),\sigma(y^{\prime}),\sigma(z))
is a square, where
\sigma
is a power of the cyclic permutation
x\to y\to z\to y^{\prime}\to x.
Therefore, we can skip
(GJM4’)
. Also note that
(GJM1)=(GJM1’)
.
So we need to prove that
(GJM1)-(GJM3)
imply
(GJM2’),(GJM3’)
.

Let us prove that
(GJM1),(GJM2)
imply
(GJM2’)
. For any triangle
(x,y,z)
the relation
(x,y)\cdot(y,z)=(x,z)
combined with
(GJM1’)
implies five permuted relations

\begin{split}(y,x)\cdot(x,z)&=(y,x)\cdot(x,y)\cdot(y,z)=(y,z),\\
(x,z)\cdot(z,y)&=(x,y)\cdot(y,z)\cdot(z,y)=(x,y),\\
(z,y)\cdot(y,x)&=((x,y)\cdot(y,z))^{-1}=(x,z)^{-1}=(z,x),\\
(z,x)\cdot(x,y)&=((y,x)\cdot(x,z))^{-1}=(y,z)^{-1}=(z,y),\\
(y,z)\cdot(z,x)&=((x,z)\cdot(z,y))^{-1}=(x,y)^{-1}=(y,x).\end{split}

Let us prove that
(GJM1),(GJM3)
imply
(GJM3’)
. If
(x,y,y^{\prime},z)
is a square the relation
(x,y)\cdot(y,z)=(x,y^{\prime})\cdot(y^{\prime},z)
combined with
(GJM1)
implies three permuted relations

\begin{split}(y,z)\cdot(z,y^{\prime})&=(y,x)\cdot(x,y)\cdot(y,z)\cdot(z,y^{\prime})\\
&=(y,x)\cdot(x,y^{\prime})\cdot(y^{\prime},z)\cdot(z,y^{\prime})=(y,x)\cdot(x,y^{\prime});\\
(z,y^{\prime})\cdot(y^{\prime},x)&=((x,y^{\prime})\cdot(y^{\prime},z))^{-1}=((x,y)\cdot(y,z))^{-1}=(z,y)\cdot(y,x);\\
(y^{\prime},x)\cdot(x,y)&=((y,x)\cdot(x,y^{\prime}))^{-1}=((y,z)\cdot(z,y^{\prime}))^{-1}=(y^{\prime},z)\cdot(z,y).\end{split}

The assertion follows.
∎

Theorem 2.4
.

The
Grigor’yan-Jimenez-Muranov fundamental groupoid is naturally isomorphic to the
2
-fundamental groupoid

(2.16)

\Pi^{\sf GJM}(X)\cong\Pi^{2}(X).

Remark 2.5
.

Grigor’yan, Jimenez and Muranov define a CW-complex
\Delta(X)
associated with
X
, whose homotopy groupoid coincides with
\Pi^{\sf GJM}(X).
In some sense
\mathscr{N}^{2}(X)
is similar to
\Delta(X).
However, in general
|\mathscr{N}^{2}(X)|
is not homotopy equivalent to
\Delta(X).
For example, one can take

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
.
In this example
\Delta(X)\sim S^{2}
and
|\mathsf{N}^{2}(X)|
is contractible.

Proof of Theorem
2.4
.

Set
\Pi^{2}:=\Pi^{2}(X)
and
\Pi^{\sf GJM}:=\Pi^{\sf GJM}(X).
Here we will use the description of
\Pi^{2}
given in Proposition
2.2
. So
\Pi^{2}
is the groupoid defined by the digraph
X
and relations (
2.11
) for
l=2.
In other words, all paths with the same tail and head of length
\leq 2
are identified. In order to prove the statement we will construct functors
\Phi:\Pi^{\sf GJM}\to\Pi^{2}
and
\Psi:\Pi^{2}\to\Pi^{\sf GJM}
such that
\Phi\circ\Psi={\sf Id}
and
\Psi\circ\Phi={\sf Id}.

The digraph
X
is a sub-digraph of
X^{\pm}.
The embedding induces a functor
\tilde{\Psi}:F(X)\to\Pi^{\sf GJM}(X).
In order to prove that
\tilde{\Psi}
induces a functor
\Psi:\Pi^{2}\to\Pi^{\sf GJM}
we need to check that the relations (
2.11
) are satisfied in
\Pi^{\sf GJM}
for
0\leq n,m\leq 2.
Without loss of generality we can assume that
n\leq m.
If
n=m=2,
this follows from
(GJM3)
. If
n=1,m=2,
this follows from
(GJM2)
. If
n=m=1,
it is obvious. If
n=0,m=2,
this follows from
(GJM1)
. If
n=0,m=1,
then
x_{0}=x_{1}=y_{0}=y_{1}
and it follows from the equation
1_{x_{0}}=(x_{0},x_{0}).
If
n=m=0,
it is obvious. Therefore,
\tilde{\Psi}
induces a functor
\Psi:\Pi^{2}\to\Pi^{\sf GJM}.

Consider a functor

\tilde{\Phi}:F(X^{\pm})\to\Pi^{2}

which is identical on objects and defined on arrows of
X^{\pm}
by the formula

(2.17)

\tilde{\Phi}((x,x^{\prime}))=\begin{cases}(x,x^{\prime})&\text{ if }(x,x^{\prime})\in X_{1}\\
(x^{\prime},x)^{-1}&\text{ if }(x^{\prime},x)\in X_{1}\text{ and }(x,x^{\prime})\notin X_{1}.\end{cases}

In order to prove that
\tilde{\Phi}
induces a functor
\Phi:\Pi^{\sf GJM}\to\Pi^{2},
we need to prove that
\tilde{\Phi}
sends all the relations
(GJM1)
-
(GJM3)
to identities.

(GJM1)
. We need to consider three cases.

(GJM1)
.1. Assume that
(x,y),(y,x)\in X_{1}.
Then
(x,y)\cdot(y,x)=(x,x)
in
\Pi^{2}
and

\tilde{\Phi}((x,y))\cdot\tilde{\Phi}((y,x))=(x,y)\cdot(y,x)=(x,x)=1_{x}.

(GJM1)
.2. Assume that
(x,y)\in X_{1}
and
(y,x)\notin X_{1}.
Then

\tilde{\Phi}((x,y)\cdot(y,x))=(x,y)\cdot(x,y)^{-1}=1_{x}.

(GJM1)
.3. The case
(x,y)\notin X_{1}
and
(y,x)\in X_{1}
is similar to
(GJM1)
.2.

(GJM2)
. If
(x,y,z)
is a triangle, then
(x,y)\cdot(y,z)=(x,y)
in
\Pi^{2}
and

\tilde{\Phi}((x,y)\cdot(y,z))=(x,y)\cdot(y,z)=(x,z)=\tilde{\Phi}((x,z)).

(GJM3)
Assume that
(x,y,y^{\prime},z)
is a square. Then
(x,y)\cdot(y,z)=(x,z)=(x,y^{\prime})\cdot(y^{\prime},z)
in
\Pi^{2}
and

\tilde{\Phi}((x,y)\cdot(y,z))=(x,y)\cdot(y,z)=(x,y^{\prime})\cdot(y^{\prime},z)=\tilde{\Phi}((x,y^{\prime})\cdot(y^{\prime},z)).

Therefore,
\tilde{\Phi}
induces a well-defined functor

\Phi:\Pi^{\sf GJM}\to\Pi^{2}.

Now we prove that
\Phi\circ\Psi={\sf Id}_{\Pi^{2}}
and
\Psi\circ\Phi={\sf Id}_{\Pi^{\sf GJM}}.
It is enough to prove these equations for arrows from generating quivers. For any
(x,x^{\prime})\in X_{1}
the equation
\Phi(\Psi((x,x^{\prime})))=(x,x^{\prime})
is obvious. Hence,
\Phi\circ\Psi={\sf Id}_{\Pi^{\sf GJM}}.
For any
(x,x^{\prime})\in X_{1}
the equation
\Psi(\Phi((x,x^{\prime})))=(x,x^{\prime})
is also obvious. If
(x,x^{\prime})\notin X_{1}
but
(x^{\prime},x)\in X_{1},
then
\Psi(\Phi((x,x^{\prime})))=\Psi((x^{\prime},x)^{-1})=(x^{\prime},x)^{-1}=(x,x^{\prime})
by
(GJM1)
.
∎

2.6.
Clusters in path chains

Let
X
be a digraph and
\mathcal{R}(X)
be the chain complex of regular accessible sequences. For two vertices
x,x^{\prime}
we denote by

\mathcal{A}_{n}(X,x,x^{\prime})
the submodule of
\mathcal{A}_{n}(X)
generated by paths with tail
x
and head
x^{\prime}.
Then

(2.18)

\mathcal{A}_{n}(X)=\bigoplus_{(x,x^{\prime})\in X_{0}^{2}}\mathcal{A}_{n}(X,x,x^{\prime}).

It is known that this decomposition induces a decomposition for the components of the complex of path chains

(2.19)

\Omega_{n}(X)=\bigoplus_{(x,x^{\prime})\in X_{0}^{2}}\Omega_{n}(X,x,x^{\prime}),

where
\Omega_{n}(X,x,x^{\prime})=\Omega_{n}(X)\cap\mathcal{A}_{n}(X,x,x^{\prime})

[
10
, Lemma 2.2]
. Elements of
\Omega_{n}(X,x,x^{\prime})
are called clusters or
(x,x^{\prime})
-clusters.

In this subsection we want to present a finer decomposition of
\Omega_{n}(X)
, which is indexed by morphisms of the
2
-fundamental groupoid. For a morphism
\gamma
in
\Pi^{2}(X)
we denote by
\mathcal{A}_{n}(X,\gamma)
the submodule of
\mathcal{A}_{n}(X)
generated by paths that represent
\gamma.
Then

(2.20)

\mathcal{A}_{n}(X,x,x^{\prime})=\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Pi^{2}(X)(x,x^{\prime})}\mathcal{A}_{n}(X,\gamma).

We consider the module
\Omega_{n}(X,\gamma)=\Omega_{n}(X)\cap\mathcal{A}_{n}(X,\gamma),
whose elements will be called
\gamma
-clusters.

Proposition 2.6
.

Any
(x,x^{\prime})
-cluster can be uniquely presented as a sum of
\gamma
-clusters

(2.21)

\Omega_{n}(X,x,x^{\prime})=\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Pi^{2}(X)(x,x^{\prime})}\Omega_{n}(X,\gamma).

Proof.

For an element
r\in\mathcal{R}_{n}(X)
and an accessible sequence
(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})
we denote by
r^{x_{0},\dots,x_{n}}\in\mathbb{K}
the coefficient of
r
at the sequence
(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})
. If
(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})
is not regular, we assume
r^{x_{0},\dots,x_{n}}=0.
Therefore,
r\in\mathcal{A}_{n}(X)
if and only if for any accessible sequence
(x_{0},\dots,x_{n}),
which is not a path, we have
r^{x_{0},\dots,x_{n}}=0.

For an element
a\in\mathcal{A}_{n}(X,x,x^{\prime})
we denote by
a_{\gamma}
its component in
\mathcal{A}_{n}(X,\gamma).
Its coefficients are defined by the formula

(2.22)

a_{\gamma}^{x_{0},\dots,x_{n}}=\begin{cases}a^{x_{0},\dots,x_{n}},&\text{ if }(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})\text{ represents }\gamma,\\
0,&\text{ else. }\end{cases}

Assume that
a\in\Omega_{n}(X,x,x^{\prime})
and
\gamma\in\Pi^{2}(X)(x,x^{\prime}).
It is sufficient to prove that
a_{\gamma}\in\Omega_{n}(X).
Here we use Proposition
1.12
for the description of
\Omega_{n}(X).
Then
d_{i}(a)\in\mathcal{A}_{n-1}(X)
for any
1\leq i\leq n-1.
Consider an accessible sequence
(y_{0},\dots,y_{n-1}),
which is not a path. Therefore we know that
d_{i}(a)^{y_{0},\dots,y_{n-1}}=0,
and we need to prove that
d_{i}(a_{\gamma})^{y_{0},\dots,y_{n-1}}=0
for any
1\leq i\leq n-1.
By the formula for
d_{i}
we see that

(2.23)

0=d_{i}(a)^{y_{0},\dots,y_{n-1}}=\sum_{z\in X_{0}}a^{y_{0},\dots,y_{i-1},z,y_{i},\dots,y_{n-1}}.

However, if
\sigma=(y_{0},\dots,y_{i-1},z,y_{i+1},\dots,y_{n})
is not a regular path, then

a^{\sigma}=0.
Therefore, if we denote by
T

(here
T
depends on
(y_{0},\dots,y_{n})
and
i
)
the set of all regular paths of the form
(y_{0},\dots,y_{i-1},

z,y_{i},\dots,y_{n})
, where
z\in X_{0},
we obtain

\sum_{t\in T}a^{t}=0.

By Proposition
2.2

for any
z,z^{\prime}
such that
(y_{i-1},z,y_{i})
and
(y_{i-1},z^{\prime},y_{i})
are paths, the paths
(y_{i-1},z,y_{i})
and
(y_{i-1},z^{\prime},y_{i})
represent the same element in
\Pi^{2}(X).
Hence, any two elements
t_{1},t_{2}\in T
also represent the same element in
\Pi^{2}(X)
. If all elements of
T
represent the element
\gamma,
then
a_{\gamma}^{t}=a^{t}
for all
t\in T.
If all elements of
T
represent some other element
\gamma^{\prime}\neq\gamma
, then
a_{\gamma}^{t}=0
for all
t\in T.
In both cases we obtain

d_{i}(a_{\gamma})^{y_{0},\dots,y_{n-1}}=\sum_{t\in T}a_{\gamma}^{t}=0.

∎

3.
Covering digraphs

3.1.
Covering quivers

Denote by
*
the one point quiver with vertex
0
, by
I
the quiver with two vertices
0,1
and one non-degenerate arrow
0\to 1.
There are two morphisms
\iota_{k}:*\to I
such that
\iota_{k}(0)=k
for
k\in\{0,1\}.
A morphism of quivers
p:E\to Q
is called covering of
X
, if for any commutative diagram

(3.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{k}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow

(with any
k\in\{0,1\}
) there exists a unique morphism
I\to E
making the diagram commutative. In other words,
p
is covering, if for any arrow of
Q
a lift of its tail or head uniquely defines a lift of the arrow. To be more precise:
p
is a covering if and only if for any
\gamma\in Q_{1}
and any
e\in p^{-1}(d_{1}(\gamma))
(resp.
e^{\prime}\in p^{-1}(d_{0}(\gamma))
) there exists a unique
\tilde{\gamma}\in E_{1}
such that
p(\tilde{\gamma})=\gamma
and
d_{1}(\tilde{\gamma})=e
(resp.
d_{0}(\tilde{\gamma})=e^{\prime}
).

For subsets
W,W^{\prime}
of vertices of a quiver
Q
we set

(3.2)

Q(W,W^{\prime})=\{\gamma\in Q_{1}\mid d_{1}(\gamma)\in W,d_{0}(\gamma)\in W^{\prime}\},

and if
W=\{q\}
or
W^{\prime}=\{q\},
we set
Q(q,W^{\prime})=Q(\{q\},W^{\prime})
and
Q(W,q)=Q(W,\{q\}).
Then the property of being covering for
p
can be also reformulated as follows: for any
e\in E_{0}
and
q\in Q_{0}
the maps

(3.3)

E(e,p^{-1}(q))\to Q(p(e),q),\hskip 28.45274ptE(p^{-1}(q),e)\to Q(q,p(e))

are bijections.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
p:E\to Q
be a covering quiver and
Q^{\prime}\subseteq Q
is a subquiver. Then the restriction
p^{-1}(Q^{\prime})\to Q^{\prime}
is also a covering quiver.

Proof.

Obvious.
∎

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
p:E\to Q
be a covering of quivers and
(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})
be a path in
Q
and
(q_{0},\dots,q_{n})
be the associated sequence of vertices in
Q_{0}
. Then for any
0\leq k\leq n
and
e\in p^{-1}(q_{k})
there exists a unique path
(\tilde{\gamma}_{1},\dots,\tilde{\gamma}_{n})
in
E
with the associated sequence of vertices
(e_{0},\dots,e_{n})
such that
p(\tilde{\gamma}_{i})=\gamma_{i}
for any
0\leq i\leq n
and
e_{k}=e.

Proof.

Obvious.
∎

3.2.
l
-covering digraphs

A morphism of digraphs
p:E\to X
is called
1
-covering, if
Q(p):Q(E)\to Q(E)
is a covering quiver.

For a natural
n
we denote by
D_{n}(X)
the digraph such that
D_{n}(X)_{0}=X_{0}
and
D_{n}(X)_{1}=\{(x,x^{\prime})\mid\mathsf{dist}(x,x^{\prime})\leq n\}.
In particular,
D_{1}(X)=X.
The digraph
D_{n}(X)
is natural by
X
and we obtain a functor

(3.4)

D_{n}:{\sf Digr}\longrightarrow{\sf Digr}.

For a natural
l
we say that a morphism of digraphs
p:E\to X
is an
l
-covering
, if
D_{n}(p):D_{n}(E)\to D_{n}(X)
is a
1
-covering for any
1\leq n\leq l.

Proposition 3.3
.

For a morphism of digraphs
p:E\to X
the following statements are equivalent.

(1)

p
is an
l
-covering of digraphs.

(2)

D_{l}(p):D_{l}(E)\to D_{l}(X)
is a
1
-covering and
E=D_{l}(p)^{-1}(X)
.

(3)

p
is a
1
-covering and, if
(e_{0},\dots,e_{n})
and
(e^{\prime}_{0},\dots,e^{\prime}_{m})
are paths in
E
such that
0\leq n,m\leq l
and either
e_{0}=e^{\prime}_{0},

p(e_{n})=p(e_{m}^{\prime})
or
p(e_{0})=p(e_{0}^{\prime}),

e_{n}=e^{\prime}_{m},
then
e_{0}=e^{\prime}_{0},e_{n}=e^{\prime}_{m}.

(4)

For any vertices
x,x^{\prime}
of
X
such that
0\leq\mathsf{dist}(x,x^{\prime})\leq l
there is a unique bijection
\alpha:p^{-1}(x)\to p^{-1}(x^{\prime})
such that for any
e\in p^{-1}(x)
we have
\mathsf{dist}(e,\alpha(e))=\mathsf{dist}(x,x^{\prime})
and
\mathsf{dist}(e,e^{\prime})>l
for any
e^{\prime}\in p^{-1}(x^{\prime})\setminus\{\alpha(e)\}.

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2).
By the assumption
D_{l}(p)
is a
1
-covering. Obviously,
E\subseteq D_{l}(p)^{-1}(X).
Prove
D_{l}(p)^{-1}(X)\subseteq E.
Take
(e,e^{\prime})\in D_{l}(p)^{-1}(X)_{1}
and set
x=p(e),x^{\prime}=p(e^{\prime}).
Then
(x,x^{\prime})\in X_{1}.
Since
p
is a
1
-covering, then there exists a unique
e^{\prime\prime}\in p^{-1}(x^{\prime})
such that
(e,e^{\prime\prime})\in E_{1}.
On the other hand
D_{l}(p)
is also
1
-covering, and hence,
e^{\prime\prime}=e^{\prime}.
Therefore
(e,e^{\prime})\in E_{1}.

(2)\Rightarrow(3).
Lemma
3.1
implies that
p
is a
1
-covering. Take two paths

(e_{0},\dots,e_{n})
and
(e^{\prime}_{0},\dots,e^{\prime}_{m})
in
E
such that
0\leq n,m\leq l
and
e_{0}=e^{\prime}_{0},

p(e_{n})=p(e_{m}^{\prime}).
Set
e:=e_{0}=e^{\prime}_{0}
and
x:=p(e),x^{\prime}:=p(e_{n})=p(e_{m}^{\prime}).

Then
\mathsf{dist}(x,x^{\prime})\leq\mathsf{dist}(e_{0},e_{n})\leq l
and
(x,x^{\prime})\in(D_{l}(X))_{1}.
Since
D_{l}(p)
is a
1
-covering, there exists a unique
e^{\prime}\in p^{-1}(x^{\prime})
such that
(e,e^{\prime})\in(D_{l}(X))_{1}.
Therefore
e_{n}=e^{\prime}=e^{\prime}_{m}.
The second case, where
p(e_{0})=p(e^{\prime}_{0})
and

e_{n}=e^{\prime}_{m},
is similar.

(3)\Rightarrow(4).
Chose a path
(x_{0},\dots,x_{n})
in
X
such that
x=x_{0}
and
x^{\prime}=x_{n}
and
\mathsf{dist}(x,x^{\prime})\leq n\leq l.
Since
p
is a
1
-covering, by Lemma
3.2
for any
e\in p^{-1}(x)
there exists a path
(e_{0},\dots,e_{n})
such that
e=e_{0}
and
p(e_{i})=x_{i}
for any
i.
Then we set
\alpha(e)=e_{n}\in p^{-1}(x^{\prime}).

By the assumption the definition of
\alpha(e)
does not depend on the choice of the path
(x_{0},\dots,x_{n}).
So, using the assumption once again, we obtain that
\alpha:p^{-1}(x)\to p^{-1}(x^{\prime})
is a well-defined map such that
\alpha(e)\in p^{-1}(x^{\prime})
is the unique vertex with the property
\mathsf{dist}(e,\alpha(e))\leq l.
Since we can take
n=\mathsf{dist}(x,x^{\prime})
in the definition of
\alpha(e),
we see that
\mathsf{dist}(e,\alpha(e))\leq\mathsf{dist}(x,x^{\prime}).
Then (
1.3
) implies
\mathsf{dist}(e,\alpha(e))=\mathsf{dist}(x,x^{\prime}).
Dually, we can define a map
\beta:p^{-1}(x^{\prime})\to p^{-1}(x)
such that
\beta(e^{\prime})\in p^{-1}(x)
is the unique vertex such that
\mathsf{dist}(\beta(e^{\prime}),e^{\prime})\leq l.
It is easy to see that
\alpha\circ\beta={\sf id}
and
\beta\circ\alpha={\sf id}.

(4)\Rightarrow(1).

Let
1\leq n\leq l
and
(x,x^{\prime})\in D_{n}(X)_{1}
and

e\in p^{-1}(x).

Then there exists a unique
e^{\prime}=\alpha(e)\in p^{-1}(x^{\prime})
such that
(e,e^{\prime})\in D_{n}(E)_{1}.

Similarly, if

(x,x^{\prime})\in D_{n}(X)_{1}
and
e^{\prime}\in p^{-1}(x^{\prime}),
then there exists a unique
e=\alpha^{-1}(e^{\prime})\in p^{-1}(x)
such that
(e,e^{\prime})\in D_{n}(E)_{1}.
Therefore
D_{n}(E)\to D_{n}(X)
is a
1
-covering for any
1\leq n\leq l
.
∎

Corollary 3.4
.

Let
p:E\to X
be an
l
-covering. Then the following holds.

•

If
e,e^{\prime}\in E_{0}
such that
\mathsf{dist}(e,e^{\prime})\leq l,
then
\mathsf{dist}(p(e),p(e^{\prime}))=\mathsf{dist}(e,e^{\prime});

•

If
e,e^{\prime}\in p^{-1}(x)
for some
x\in X_{0}
and
e\neq e^{\prime},
then
\mathsf{dist}(e,e^{\prime})>l.

Corollary 3.5
.

Let
p:E\to X
be an
l
-covering of digraphs and
(x_{0},\dots,x_{m})
be an accessible sequence in
X
such that
\mathsf{dist}(x_{i},x_{i+1})\leq l.
Then for any
0\leq k\leq m
and
e\in p^{-1}(x_{k})
there exists a unique accessible sequence
(e_{0},\dots,e_{m})
in
E
such that
\mathsf{dist}(e_{i},e_{i+1})\leq l,

p(e_{i})=x_{i}
for any
0\leq i\leq m
and
e_{k}=e.
Moreover, in this case
\mathsf{dist}(e_{i},e_{i+1})=\mathsf{dist}(x_{i},x_{i+1}).

3.3.
Relation to covering simplicial sets

In this section we will use theory of covering simplicial sets
[
6
, Appendix I]
. We denote by
\Delta
the category of non-empty finite ordinals and by
\Delta^{m}
the standard
m
-simplex
\Delta^{m}=\Delta(-,[m]).
For
0\leq k\leq m
we consider a map
i_{k}:[0]\to[m]
that sends
0
to
k.
We use the same notation for the induced map
i_{k}:\Delta^{0}\to\Delta^{m}.
A morphism of simplicial sets
\mathpzc{p}:\mathpzc{E}\to\mathpzc{X}
is called covering, if for any diagram

(3.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathpzc{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathpzc{X}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{k}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathpzc{p}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow

(with any
0\leq k\leq m
)
there exists a unique morphism
\Delta^{m}\to\mathpzc{E}
such that the diagram is commutative.

Proposition 3.6
.

The following properties of a morphism of digraphs
p:E\to X
are equivalent.

(1)

p:E\to X
is an
l
-covering.

(2)

\mathscr{N}^{n}(p):\mathscr{N}^{n}(E)\to\mathscr{N}^{n}(X)
is a covering of simplicial sets for any
1\leq n\leq l.

(3)

\mathscr{N}^{l}(p):\mathscr{N}^{l}(E)\to\mathscr{N}^{l}(X)
is a covering of simplicial sets and
\mathscr{N}^{1}(E)=\mathscr{N}^{l}(p)^{-1}(\mathscr{N}^{1}(X)).

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2).
It is sufficient to prove that for any natural
m\geq 1,
any
0\leq k\leq m,
any
(x_{0},\dots,x_{m})\in\mathscr{N}^{n}(X)_{m}
and any
e\in p^{-1}(x_{k})
there exists a unique
(e_{0},\dots,e_{m})\in\mathscr{N}^{n}(E)_{m}
such that

p(e_{i})=x_{i}

and
e_{k}=e.
Since
\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\mathsf{dist}(x_{i},x_{i+1})\leq n,
we have
\mathsf{dist}(x_{i},x_{i+1})\leq n
for each
0\leq i<m,
and hence,
(x_{0},\dots,x_{m})
is a path in
D_{n}(X).

Using that
D_{n}(E)\to D_{n}(X)
is a covering, by Lemma
3.2
we obtain that there exists a unique path
(e_{0},\dots,e_{n})
in
D_{n}(E)
such that
e_{k}=e
and
p(e_{i})=x_{i}.

Since
\mathsf{dist}(e_{i},e_{i+1})\leq n,
by Corollary
3.4
we obtain that
\mathsf{dist}(e_{i},e_{i+1})=\mathsf{dist}(x_{i},x_{i+1}).
Hence
(e_{0},\dots,e_{m})\in\mathscr{N}^{n}(E)_{m}.
The uniqueness of the lifting to
\mathscr{N}^{n}(E)_{m}
follows from the uniqueness of the lifting to
D_{n}(E).

(2)\Rightarrow(3).
By the assumption
D_{l}(X)
is a covering. Prove that
\mathscr{N}^{1}(E)=(\mathscr{N}^{l}(p))^{-1}(\mathscr{N}^{1}(X)).
Take
(e_{0},\dots,e_{m})\in\mathscr{N}^{l}(E)_{m}
such that
(p(e_{0}),\dots,p(e_{m}))\in\mathscr{N}^{1}(X)_{m}.
Since
\mathscr{N}^{1}(p)
is a covering and
(e_{0})\in\mathscr{N}^{1}(E)_{0}
, there exists a unique
(e^{\prime}_{0},\dots,e^{\prime}_{m})\in\mathscr{N}^{1}(E)_{m}
such that
e^{\prime}_{0}=e_{0}
and
p(e^{\prime}_{i})=p(e_{i}).
Using that
\mathscr{N}^{l}(p)
is a covering, we obtain
(e_{0},\dots,e_{m})=(e^{\prime}_{0},\dots,e^{\prime}_{m}).
Therefore,
(e_{0},\dots,e_{m})\in\mathscr{N}^{1}(E)_{m}.
Hence
\mathscr{N}^{1}(E)=(\mathscr{N}^{l}(p))^{-1}(\mathscr{N}^{1}(X)).

(3)\Rightarrow(1).
Note that

D_{n}(X)_{1}=\mathscr{N}^{n}(X)_{1},

D_{n}(E)_{1}=\mathscr{N}^{n}(E)_{1}.
Then the fact that
\mathscr{N}^{l}(p)
is a covering implies that
D_{l}(p)
is a
1
-covering and the fact that
\mathscr{N}^{1}(E)=(\mathscr{N}^{l}(p))^{-1}(\mathscr{N}^{1}(X))
implies that
E=D_{l}(p)^{-1}(X).
Then Proposition
3.3
implies that
p
is an
l
-covering.
∎

3.4.
Covering groupoids

Here we remind some aspects of the theory of covering groupoids that can be found in
[
6
, Appendix I]
,
[
19
]
and
[
4
]
. We will also give some well known results with proofs because it is difficult to find a precise reference.

Any groupoid (as well as any category) can be treated as a quiver, if we forget the composition map. We say that a morphism of groupoids
p:\mathpzc{H}\to\mathpzc{G}
is a covering, if it is a covering quiver.

Example 3.7
.

If
p:E\to Q
is a covering quiver, then the morphism between the free groupoids
F(p):F(E)\to F(Q)
is a covering groupoid.

For a groupoid
\mathpzc{G}
we denote by
{\sf Cov}(\mathpzc{G})
the full subcategory of
{\sf Grpd}\!\downarrow\!\mathpzc{G}
consisting of coverings of the groupoid
\mathpzc{G}
. The category of coverings of a groupoid is equivalent to the category of
\mathpzc{G}
-presheaves

(3.6)

{\sf Cov}(\mathpzc{G})\simeq{\sf Set}^{\mathpzc{G}^{\sf op}}

[
6
, Appendix I.1.2]
. The equivalence

{\sf f}:{\sf Cov}(\mathpzc{G})\to{\sf Set}^{\mathpzc{G}^{\sf op}}
is defined so that
{\sf f}(p)(g)=p^{-1}(g)
for
g\in{\sf Ob}(\mathpzc{G})
and for a morphism
\gamma:g^{\prime}\to g
in
\mathpzc{G}
the map
{\sf f}(p)(\gamma):p^{-1}(g)\to p^{-1}(g^{\prime})
is defined by the unique lifting property:
h^{\prime}={\sf f}(p)(\gamma)(h)
is the only object such that there exists a morphism
\tilde{\gamma}:h^{\prime}\to h
with
p(\tilde{\gamma})=\gamma.
We will use the following notation

(3.7)

h\gamma:={\sf f}(p)(\gamma)(h).

For an object
g_{0}
of
\mathpzc{G}
we set
\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G},g_{0})=\mathpzc{G}(g_{0},g_{0}).
We can treat
\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G},g_{0})
as a groupoid with one object
g_{0}.
Then the restriction of
{\sf f}(p)
on
\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G},g_{0})
defines a right
\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G},g_{0})
-set
{\sf f}_{g_{0}}(p)=p^{-1}(g_{0}).

For a group
G
we denote by
G\text{-}{\sf Set}
the category of right
G
-sets. Note that, if we treat a group as a groupoid with one object, then
G\text{-}{\sf Set}
is isomorphic to the category of
G
-presheaves.

Proposition 3.8
.

If
\mathpzc{G}
is a connected groupoid and
g_{0}
is its object, then there is an equivalence of categories

(3.8)

{\sf Cov}(\mathpzc{G})\simeq\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G},g_{0})\text{-}{\sf Set},

that sends
p
to
{\sf f}_{g_{0}}(p).
Moreover, this equivalence can be restricted to an equivalence between the category of connected covering groupoids and the category of transitive
\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G},g_{0})
-sets

(3.9)

{\sf Cov}^{\sf conn}(\mathpzc{G})\simeq\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G},g_{0})\text{-}{\sf Set}^{\sf trans}.

Proof.

Since
\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G},g_{0})\hookrightarrow\mathpzc{G}
is an equivalence of categories, the restriction functor
{\sf Set}^{\mathpzc{G}^{\sf op}}\to{\sf Set}^{\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G},g_{0})^{\sf op}}=\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G},g_{0})\text{-}{\sf Set}
is also an equivalence. The equivalence (
3.8
) follows. In both categories
{\sf Cov}(\mathpzc{G})
and
\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G})\text{-}{\sf Set}
coproducts are defined by disjoint unions. So, the disjoint union in
{\sf Cov}(\mathpzc{G})
correspond the disjoint union in
\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G})\text{-}{\sf Set}.
Connected coverings are coverings indecomposable to disjoint union of nontrivial coverings, and transitive
\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G})
-sets are also indecomposable
\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G})
-sets into disjoint union. Then they correspond to each other under this equivalence.
∎

A pointed groupoid is a couple
(\mathpzc{G},g_{0}),
where
\mathpzc{G}
is a groupoid and
g_{0}
is its object, which is called the basepoint. By abuse of notation we set
\mathpzc{G}=(\mathpzc{G},g_{0})
and
\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G})=\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G},g_{0}).
A morphism of pointed groupoids is a morphism of groupoids that takes the basepoint to the basepoint. The category of pointed groupoids is denoted by
{\sf Grpd}_{*}.
A pointed covering is a morphism of pointed groupoids, which is a covering. The category of pointed coverings
{\sf Cov}_{*}(\mathpzc{G})
is a full subcategory in the comma category
{\sf Grpd}_{*}\!\downarrow\!\mathpzc{G},
whose objects are pointed coverings. The category of connected pointed coverings is denoted by
{\sf Cov}_{*}^{\sf conn}(\mathpzc{G}).

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
p:\mathpzc{H}\to\mathpzc{G}
be a pointed covering of
\mathpzc{G}.
Then the morphism
p_{*}:\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{H})\to\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G})
is injective and the image is equal to the stabilizer of the object
h_{0}
with respect to the action of
\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G})
on
p^{-1}(g_{0})

p_{*}(\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{H}))={\sf Stab}(\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G}),h_{0}).

Proof.

Since
\tilde{\gamma}:h_{0}\gamma\to h_{0}
from the definition of the
\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G})
-action on
{\sf f}_{g_{0}}
is unique, we obtain that the map
p_{*}:\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{H})\to\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G})
is injective. We can also note that
\tilde{\gamma}\in\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{H})
if and only if
h_{0}\gamma=h_{0}.
The formula with the stabiliser follows.
∎

For a group
G
we denote by
{\sf Sub}(G)
the category, whose objects are subgroups of
G
and morphisms are embeddings.

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
\mathpzc{G}
be a pointed connected groupoid. Then there is an equivalence of categories

(3.10)

{\sf Cov}_{*}^{\sf conn}(\mathpzc{G})\simeq{\sf Sub}(\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G}))

that sends a pointed covering
p:\mathpzc{H}\to\mathpzc{G}
to the image of
p_{*}:\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{H})\to\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G}).
Moreover, for any pointed connected covering
p
there is an isomorphism
{\sf f}_{g_{0}}(p)\cong\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G})/p_{*}(\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{H}))
of
\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G})
-sets.

Proof.

The equivalence
{\sf f}_{g_{0}}
(
3.9
) implies that there is an equivalence between the category
{\sf Cov}_{*}^{\sf conn}(\mathpzc{G})
and the category of transitive
\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G})
-sets with a fixed basepoint
(\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G})\text{-}{\sf Set}^{\sf trans})_{*}
. Objects in this category are couples
(S,s_{0}),
where
S
is a transitive
\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G})
-set and
s_{0}\in S
(we don’t assume that the action preserves
s_{0}
).
Morphisms of this category are morphisms of
\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G})
-sets that preserve the basepoint. Any such a transitive
\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G})
-set
S
is isomorphic to
\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G})/H,
where
H={\sf Stab}_{S}(\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G}),s_{0}).

Since for any morphism
f:(S,s_{0})\to(S^{\prime},s_{0}^{\prime})
we have
f(s_{0})=s^{\prime}_{0},
we obtain that it can be only unique (because a morphism between transitive
G
-sets is defined by the value on one element) and it exists only if
{\sf Stab}_{S}(\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G}),s_{0})\subseteq{\sf Stab}_{S^{\prime}}(\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G}),s^{\prime}_{0}).
Therefore the functor
(\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G})\text{-}{\sf Set}^{\sf trans})_{*}\to{\sf Sub}(\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G})),(S,s_{0})\mapsto{\sf Stab}_{S}(\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G}),s_{0})
is an equivalence. Lemma
3.9
implies that the composite equivalence
{\sf Cov}_{*}^{\sf conn}(\mathpzc{G})\to{\sf Sub}(\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G}))
can be described as
(p:\mathpzc{H}\to\mathpzc{G})\mapsto p_{*}(\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G})),
and that
{\sf f}_{g_{0}}(p)\cong\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{G})/p_{*}(\pi_{1}(\mathpzc{H})).

∎

3.5.
Category of
l
-covering digraphs

For a simplicial set
\mathpzc{X}
we denote by
{\sf Cov}(\mathpzc{X})
the full subcategory of the comma category
{\sf SSet}\!\downarrow\!\mathpzc{X}
consisting of the coverings of
\mathpzc{X}.
The category of coverings of a simplicial set is equivalent to the category of of coverings of its fundamental groupoid
{\sf Cov}(\mathpzc{X})\simeq{\sf Cov}(\Pi(\mathpzc{X}))

[
6
, Appendix I.2.3]
. The equivalence
{\sf Cov}(\mathpzc{X})\to{\sf Cov}(\Pi(\mathpzc{X}))
is defined by taking the fundamental groupoid
\Pi.
Therefore, for a simplicial set
\mathpzc{X}
we have equivalences

(3.11)

{\sf Cov}(\mathpzc{X})\simeq{\sf Cov}(\Pi(\mathpzc{X}))\simeq{\sf Set}^{\Pi(\mathpzc{X})^{\sf op}}.

For a digraph
X
we denote by
{\sf Cov}_{l}(X)
the full subcategory of the comma category
{\sf Digr}\!\downarrow\!X
that consists of
l
-coverings.

Theorem 3.11
.

For a digraph
X
there are equivalences of categories

(3.12)

{\sf Cov}_{l}(X)\simeq{\sf Cov}(\mathscr{N}^{l}(X))\simeq{\sf Cov}(\Pi^{l}(X))\simeq{\sf Set}^{\Pi^{l}(X)^{\sf op}}

such that the following holds.

•

The equivalence
{\sf Cov}_{l}(X)\to{\sf Cov}(\mathscr{N}^{l}(X))
is the functor that sends
p
to
\mathscr{N}^{l}(p)
and other equivalences are described above (
3.11
), (
3.6
).

•

The composite functor

{\sf Fib}:{\sf Cov}_{l}(X)\to{\sf Set}^{\Pi^{l}(X)^{\sf op}}

is defined by
{\sf Fib}(p)(x)=p^{-1}(x)
for any
x\in X_{0};
and for any arrow
(x^{\prime},x)
the map
{\sf Fib}(p)((x^{\prime},x)):p^{-1}(x)\to p^{-1}(x^{\prime})
is defined by the unique lifting property from the definition of the
1
-covering.

•

The opposite composite functor

\Psi:{\sf Set}^{\Pi^{l}(X)^{\sf op}}\longrightarrow{\sf Cov}_{l}(X)

is defined so that
\Psi(L)=(p:E\to X)
, where
E_{0}=\coprod_{x\in X_{0}}L(x);
arrows of
E
have the form
(L((x^{\prime},x))(e),e)
for an arrow
(x^{\prime},x)
of
X
and
e\in L(x).

Proof.

It is easy to see that the composition of the functor
(-)^{l}:{\sf Cov}_{l}(X)\to{\sf Cov}(\mathscr{N}^{l}(X))
with the equivalence
{\sf Cov}(\mathscr{N}^{l}(X))\simeq{\sf Cov}(\Pi^{l}(X))\simeq{\sf Set}^{\Pi^{l}(X)^{\sf op}}
is the functor
{\sf Fib}:{\sf Cov}_{l}(X)\to{\sf Set}^{\Pi^{l}(X)^{\sf op}}
described in the statement. Therefore, it is sufficient to prove that the functor
\Psi:{\sf Set}^{\Pi^{l}(X)^{\sf op}}\to{\sf Cov}_{l}(X)
is well defined and
\Psi\circ{\sf Fib}\cong{\sf Id}
and
{\sf Fib}\circ\Psi\cong{\sf Id}.

We need to prove that the morphism of digraphs
\Psi(L)=(p:E\to X)
defined in the statement is an
l
-covering. Note that
p^{-1}(x)=L(x)
and by the definition for any
(x,x^{\prime})\in X_{1}
we have a bijection
\alpha_{x,x^{\prime}}:=L((x,x^{\prime}))^{-1}:p^{-1}(x)\to p^{-1}(x^{\prime})
such that
\alpha_{x,x^{\prime}}(e)\in p^{-1}(x)
is a unique vertex such that
(e,\alpha_{x,x^{\prime}}(e))\in E_{1}.
Then
p
is a
1
-covering. Assume that we have two paths
(e_{0},\dots,e_{n})
and
(e^{\prime}_{0},\dots,e^{\prime}_{m})
in
E
such that
n,m\leq l
and
e_{n}=e^{\prime}_{m},p(e_{0})=p(e^{\prime}_{0}).
Set
x_{i}=p(e_{i}),x_{i}^{\prime}=p(e^{\prime}_{i}).
Then, using the relation (
2.11
), we obtain

(3.13)

\begin{split}e_{0}&=L((x_{0},x_{1})\cdot{\dots}\cdot(x_{n-1},x_{n}))(e_{n})\\
&=L((x^{\prime}_{0},x^{\prime}_{1})\cdot{\dots}\cdot(x^{\prime}_{m-1},x^{\prime}_{m}))(e^{\prime}_{m})=e^{\prime}_{0}.\end{split}

Similarly we prove that if we have two paths
(e_{0},\dots,e_{n})
and
(e^{\prime}_{0},\dots,e^{\prime}_{m})
in
E
such that
n,m\leq l
and
e_{0}=e^{\prime}_{0},p(e_{n})=p(e^{\prime}_{m}),
then
e_{n}=e^{\prime}_{m}.
Therefore, by Proposition
3.3

p
is an
l
-covering. The isomorphisms
{\sf Fib}\circ\Psi\cong{\sf Id}
and
\Psi\circ{\sf Fib}\cong{\sf Id}
are straightforward.
∎

A pointed digraph is a couple
(X,x_{0}),
where
X
is a digraph and
x_{0}
is its vertex. A morphism of pointed digraphs
f:(X,x_{0})\to(Y,y_{0})
is a morphism of digraphs such that
f(x_{0})=y_{0}.
This defines a category of pointed digraphs
{\sf Digr}_{*}.
By abuse of notation we will denote
X=(X,x_{0})
and set

(3.14)

\pi^{l}_{1}(X)=\pi_{1}(\Pi^{l}(X),x_{0}).

Corollary 3.12
.

If
X
is a connected pointed digraph, then there is an equivalence of categories

(3.15)

{\sf Cov}_{l}(X)\simeq\pi_{1}^{l}(X)\text{-}{\sf Set}

that sends
p
to
p^{-1}(x_{0}).

Proof.

It follows from Theorem
3.11
and
Proposition
3.8
.
∎

A digraph is called connected, if the underlying undirected graph is connected. Let
X
be a pointed connected digraph. A pointed
l
-covering
p:E\to X
is an
l
-covering, which is a morphism of pointed spaces. The category of pointed
l
-coverings
{\sf Cov}_{*,l}(X)
is the full subcategory of
{\sf Digr}_{*}\!\downarrow\!X.
The category of pointed and connected
l
-coverings will be denoted by
{\sf Cov}^{\sf conn}_{*,l}(X).

Theorem 3.13
.

Let
X
be a connected pointed digraph. Then there is an equivalence of categories

{\sf Cov}^{\sf conn}_{*,l}(X)\simeq{\sf Sub}(\pi^{l}_{1}(X))

that sends a connected pointed
l
-covering
p:E\to X
to the image of the homomorphism
p_{*}:\pi^{l}_{1}(E)\to\pi^{l}_{1}(X).
Moreover,
for any connected pointed
l
-covering
p:E\to X
the homomorphism
p_{*}:\pi^{l}_{1}(E)\to\pi^{l}_{1}(X)
is injective and there is an isomorphism
p^{-1}(x_{0})\cong\pi_{1}^{l}(X)/p_{*}(\pi_{1}^{l}(E))
of
\pi_{1}^{l}(X)
-sets.

Proof.

Theorem
3.11
implies that
{\sf Cov}^{\sf conn}_{*,l}(X)
is equivalent to the category pointed connected covering groupoids
{\sf Cov}^{\sf conn}_{*}(\Pi^{l}(X)).
Then the assertion follows from Proposition
3.10
and Lemma
3.9
.
∎

3.6.
Universal
l
-covering and Deck transformations

Let
X
be a connected digraph.
An
l
-covering

p_{U}:U\to X
is
called universal, if

\Pi^{l}(U)
is indiscrete (i.e. all hom-sets are one-element). In other words,
p_{U}:U\to X
is universal if
U
is connected and
\pi_{1}(U)
is trivial (since
U
is connected
\pi_{1}(U)
does not depend on the choice of the basepoint in
U
up to isomorphism). By Theorem

3.13
the universal covering exists, and if we fix basepoints of

X
and
U,
then

p_{U}:U\to X
is the initial
object of
{\sf Cov}^{\sf conn}_{*,l}(X).
In particular, it is unique. Moreover, if we fix a basepoint it is unique up to unique isomorphism, but if we don’t fix a basepoint, the universal covering has non-trivial automorphisms which we are going to describe. In the case of spaces such automorphisms are called Deck transformations.

If
p_{U}:U\to X
is a universal
l
-cover, for two vertices
u,u^{\prime}
of
U
we denote by
\alpha_{u,u^{\prime}}
the unique morphism in
\Pi^{l}(U)(u,u^{\prime}).

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
X
be a connected digraph and let
p_{U}:U\to X
be the universal
l
-covering with some chosen basepoints
u_{0}
in
U
and
x_{0}=p(u_{0})
in
X.
Then there is a bijection

(3.16)

\theta:U_{0}\cong\Pi^{l}(X)(X_{0},x_{0})

defined by the formulas

(3.17)

\theta(u)=p(\alpha_{u,u_{0}}),\hskip 28.45274pt\theta^{-1}(\gamma)=u_{0}\gamma.

Moreover,
(u,u^{\prime})
is an arrow in
U
if and only if
(p(u),p(u^{\prime}))
is an arrow in
X
and
(p(u),p(u^{\prime}))\cdot\theta(u^{\prime})=\theta(u).

Proof.

Since
\Pi^{l}(U)
is indiscrete, the map
p^{-1}(x)\to\Pi^{l}(U)(p^{-1}(x),u_{0})
sending
u
to
\alpha_{u,u_{0}}
is a bijection. Since

\Pi^{l}(p):\Pi^{l}(U)\to\Pi^{l}(X)

is a covering of groupoids,
p
induces a bijection
\Pi^{l}(U)(p^{-1}(x),u_{0})\cong\Pi^{l}(X)(x,x_{0}).
Therefore, we obtain that
\theta
gives a bijection
p^{-1}(x)\cong\Pi^{l}(X)(x,x_{0}).
Taking the union by all
x,
we obtain the bijection
U_{0}\cong\Pi^{l}(X)(X_{0},x_{0}).

The description of arrows in
U
follows from the definition of
1
-cover.
∎

Remark 3.15
.

Proposition
3.14
can be considered as a recipe for explicit construction of the universal
l
-cover. Define
U
so that
U_{0}=\Pi^{l}(X)(X_{0},x_{0}),
