and arrows of
U
have the form
((x,x^{\prime})\cdot\gamma,\gamma)
for
\gamma\in U_{0}
and an arrow
(x,x^{\prime}).
The map
p_{U}
is defined by the formula
p_{U}(\gamma:x\to x_{0})=x.

Proposition 3.16

(Deck transformations)
.

Let
X
be a connected digraph and let
p_{U}:U\to X
be the universal
l
-covering with chosen basepoints
u_{0}
in
U
and
x_{0}=p(u_{0})
in
X
. Then there exists a left action of
\pi_{1}^{l}(X)
on
U
(which is a right
(\pi_{1}^{l}(X),\cdot)
-action) by automorphisms of covers defined by the formula

(3.18)

\gamma u:=\theta^{-1}(\gamma\theta(u)).

This action induces an isomorphism

(3.19)

\pi^{l}_{1}(X)\cong{\sf Aut}_{{\sf Cov}_{l}(X)}(p_{U}).

Proof.

It is easy to check that action on
U
defined so that
\theta
is an isomorphism of
\pi_{1}^{l}(X)
-sets is an action of
\pi_{1}^{l}(X)
by automorphisms of covers. The action on the fibre
p^{-1}_{U}(x_{0})
is isomorphic to the action of
\pi_{1}^{l}(X)
on itself by shifts. For any group
G
there is an isomorphisms
G\cong{\sf Aut}_{G\text{-}{\sf Set}}(G)
such that
g\mapsto(x\mapsto gx).
Combining this with Corollary
3.12
we obtain that the action induces an isomorphism
\pi_{1}^{l}(X)\cong{\sf Aut}_{{\sf Cov}_{l}(X)}(p_{U}).

∎

3.7.
Homotopy lifting property for 2-covering digraphs

For two morphisms of digraphs
f,g:X\to Y
we define the distance
\mathsf{dist}(f,g)
as the supremum of the distances
\mathsf{dist}(f(x),g(x)),x\in X_{0}.
We say that two morphisms of digraphs
f,g:X\to Y
are one-step homotopic, if either
\mathsf{dist}(f,g)\leq 1,
or
\mathsf{dist}(g,f)\leq 1.
A sequence of morphisms
(f_{0},\dots,f_{n}),f_{i}:X\to Y
is called a homotopy, if
f_{i}
is one-step homotopic to
f_{i+1}.
Two morphisms
f,g:X\to Y
are homotopic, if there is a homotopy
(f_{0},\dots,f_{n})
such that
f_{0}=f,f_{n}=g.
It is easy to see that a homotopy
h=(f_{0},\dots,f_{n})
can be also defined as a morphism

(3.20)

h:X\square I_{n}\longrightarrow Y,

where
I_{n}
is a digraph with the vertex set
\{0,\dots,n\}
and one arrow between
i
and
i+1
with some choice of directions.

I_{n}:\hskip 28.45274pt0\to 1\leftarrow 2\leftarrow 3\to 4\leftarrow\dots\to n

Proposition 3.17
.

Let
p:E\to Y
be a
2
-covering digraph and let
(f_{0},\dots,f_{n}),f_{i}:X\to Y
be a homotopy. Then for any morphism
g_{0}:X\to E
such that
pg_{0}=f_{0}
there exists a unique homotopy
(g_{0},\dots,g_{n})
such that
pg_{i}=f_{i}.

Proof.

It is easy to see that it is sufficient to prove this for the case
n=1.
Assume that
f_{0},f_{1}:X\to Y
are one-step homotopic and
g_{0}:X\to E
is a morphism such that
pg_{0}=f_{0}.
So, either
\mathsf{dist}(f_{0},f_{1})\leq 1,
or
\mathsf{dist}(f_{1},f_{0})\leq 1.
Further we assume that
\mathsf{dist}(f_{0},f_{1})\leq 1,
the second case is similar. Using that
p
is a
1
-covering we obtain that for each
x
there a unique vertex
g_{1}(x)\in p^{-1}(f_{1}(x))
such that
\mathsf{dist}(g_{0}(x),g_{1}(x))=\mathsf{dist}(f_{0}(x),f_{1}(x))\leq 1.

Let us prove that
g_{1}
is a morphism
X\to E.
Assume that
\mathsf{dist}(x,x^{\prime})=1.
Then there exists a unique
e^{\prime}\in p^{-1}(f_{1}(x^{\prime}))
such that
\mathsf{dist}(g_{1}(x),e^{\prime})=\mathsf{dist}(f_{1}(x),f_{1}(x^{\prime}))\leq 1.
Then
(g_{0}(x),g_{0}(x^{\prime}),g_{1}(x^{\prime}))
and
(g_{0}(x),g_{1}(x),e^{\prime})
are
2
-paths such
p(g_{1}(x^{\prime}))=p(e^{\prime})
. By Proposition
3.3
we obtain
g_{1}(x^{\prime})=e^{\prime}
(here we use that
p
is a
2
-covering). Therefore,
\mathsf{dist}(g_{1}(x),g_{1}(x^{\prime}))=\mathsf{dist}(f_{1}(x),f_{1}(x^{\prime}))\leq 1.
Hence
g_{1}
is a morphism.

By the construction it is obvious that
g_{1}:X\to E
is the unique morphism such that
\mathsf{dist}(g_{0},g_{1})\leq 1
and
pg_{1}=f_{1}.
We need to check that, if
g^{\prime}_{1}:X\to E
is a morphism such that
pg^{\prime}_{1}=f_{1}
and
\mathsf{dist}(g_{1}^{\prime},g_{0})\leq 1,
then
g_{1}=g^{\prime}_{1}.
Indeed, using the triangle inequality and the inequalities
\mathsf{dist}(g^{\prime}_{1}(x),g_{0}(x))\leq 1
and
\mathsf{dist}(g_{0}(x),g_{1}(x))\leq 1,
we obtain
\mathsf{dist}(g^{\prime}_{1}(x),g_{1}(x))\leq\mathsf{dist}(g^{\prime}_{1}(x),g_{0}(x))+\mathsf{dist}(g_{0}(x),g_{1}(x))\leq 2.
Since
pg_{1}=f_{1}=pg^{\prime}_{1},
we have
p(g_{1}(x))=p(g^{\prime}_{1}(x)).
By Corollary
3.4
we obtain
g_{1}(x)=g^{\prime}_{1}(x).

∎

3.8.
Path chains of 2-covering digraphs

Recall that for a digraph
X
and two its vertices
x,x^{\prime}
we denote by
\Omega_{n}(X,x,x^{\prime})
the set of path chains which are linear combinations of paths with tail in
x
and head in
x^{\prime}.
More generally, for a subset in the set of vertices
W,W^{\prime}\subseteq X_{0},
we denote by
\Omega_{n}(X,W,W^{\prime})\subseteq\Omega_{n}(X)
the set of path chains which are linear combinations of paths with tail in a vertex of
W
and head in a vertex of
W^{\prime}.
The formula (
2.19
) implies that

(3.21)

\Omega_{n}(X,W,W^{\prime})=\bigoplus_{(x,x^{\prime})\in W\times W^{\prime}}\Omega_{n}(X,x,x^{\prime}).

Mainly, we will use the case when one of the sets is one-element
W=\{x\}
or
W^{\prime}=\{x^{\prime}\}
and use the notation
\Omega_{n}(X,x,W^{\prime})=\Omega_{n}(X,\{x\},W^{\prime})
and
\Omega_{n}(X,W,x^{\prime})=\Omega_{n}(X,W,\{x^{\prime}\})
in this case. Similarly we define
\mathcal{A}_{n}(X,W,W^{\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{n}(X)
and
\mathcal{R}_{n}(X,W,W^{\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{n}(X).

Proposition 3.18
.

Let
p:E\to X
be a 2-covering,
x
be a vertex of
X
and
e
be a vertex of
E.
Then
p
induces isomorphisms

(3.22)

\Omega_{n}(E,e,p^{-1}(x))\cong\Omega_{n}(X,p(e),x)

and

(3.23)

\Omega_{n}(E,p^{-1}(x),e)\cong\Omega_{n}(X,x,p(e)).

Proof.

First we introduce some notations and make some notes for arbitrary digraph
Y.

For sets of vertices
W,W^{\prime}\subseteq Y_{0}
we denote by
RP_{n}(Y,W,W^{\prime})
the set of regular paths
(y_{0},\dots,y_{n})
(i.e.
\mathsf{dist}(y_{i},y_{i+1})=1
) such that
y_{0}\in W
and
y_{n}\in W^{\prime}.
For a number
1\leq k\leq n
we denote by
RN_{n}^{k}(Y,W,W^{\prime})
the set of sequences
(y_{0},\dots,y_{n})
such that
y\in W,y^{\prime}\in W^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{dist}(y_{i},y_{i+1})=1
for
i\neq k
and
\mathsf{dist}(y_{k},y_{k+1})=2.
Note that
\mathcal{A}_{n}(Y,W,W^{\prime})
is a submodule in
\mathcal{R}_{n}(Y,W,W^{\prime})
generated by
RP_{n}(Y,W,W^{\prime}).

We denote by
B_{n}^{k}(Y,W,W^{\prime})
the submodule of
\mathcal{R}_{n}(Y,W,W^{\prime})
generated by
RN^{k}_{n}(Y,W,W^{\prime}).

Note that for any
1\leq k\leq n-1
(here we assume
k\neq 0,n
) we have
d_{k}(\mathcal{A}_{n}(Y,W,W^{\prime}))\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{n-1}(Y,W,W^{\prime})\oplus B_{n-1}^{k}(Y,W,W^{\prime}).
We denote by

(3.24)

d_{k}^{\prime}:\mathcal{A}_{n}(Y,W,W^{\prime})\to B_{n-1}^{k}(Y,W,W^{\prime})

the composition of
d_{k}
with the projection to
B_{n-1}^{k}(Y,W,W^{\prime})
. Then by Proposition
1.12
we have

(3.25)

\Omega_{n}(Y,W,W^{\prime})=\{a\in\mathcal{A}_{n}(Y,W,W^{\prime})\mid d_{k}^{\prime}(a)=0\text{ for }1\leq k\leq n-1\}.

Now assume that
p:E\to X
is a 2-covering,
x
is a vertex of
X
and
e
is a vertex of
E.
Lemma
3.2
and Corollary
3.5
imply that

p
induces bijections

(3.26)

RP_{n}(E,e,p^{-1}(x))\cong RP_{n}(X,p(e),x),

(3.27)

RN^{k}_{n}(E,e,p^{-1}(x))\cong RN^{k}_{n}(X,p(e),x).

Therefore we obtain that the horizontal arrows of the following commutative diagram are isomorphisms

(3.28)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{n}(E,e,p^{-1}(x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}_{n}(X,p(e),x)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{k}_{n-1}(E,e,p^{-1}(x))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{k}_{n-1}(X,p(e),x).}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}_{k}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{\prime}_{k}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

The first isomorphism of the statement follows from this diagram and formula (
3.25
). The second isomorphism is similar.
∎

Proposition 3.19
.

Let
p:E\to X
be a 2-covering and
\gamma
be a morphism in
\Pi^{2}(E).
Then
p
induces an isomorphism of modules of clusters

(3.29)

\Omega_{n}(E,\gamma)\cong\Omega_{n}(X,p(\gamma)).

Proof.

Combining
Proposition
3.18
and Proposition
2.6
for any vertices
x,x^{\prime}
of
X
and
e\in p^{-1}(x)
we obtain that
p
induces an isomorphism

(3.30)

\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Pi^{2}(E)(e,p^{-1}(x^{\prime}))}\Omega_{n}(E,\gamma)\cong\bigoplus_{\beta\in\Pi^{2}(X)(x,x^{\prime})}\Omega_{n}(X,\beta).

Since
\Pi^{2}(E)\to\Pi^{2}(X)
is a covering of groupoids,
\Pi^{2}(E)(e,p^{-1}(x^{\prime}))\to\Pi^{2}(X)(x,x^{\prime})
is a bijection. Therefore, the summands are in one-to-one correspondence to each other. Using that the image of
\Omega_{n}(E,\gamma)
lies in
\Omega_{n}(X,p(\gamma)),
we obtain the assertion.
∎

3.9.
Spectral sequence of the universal 2-cover

Let
X
be a connected digraph and
p_{U}:U\to X
be the universal
2
-covering. Since for any fixed basepoint we have an isomorphism (Proposition
3.16
)

\pi_{1}^{2}(X)\cong{\sf Aut}_{{\sf Cov}_{2}(X)}(p_{U}),

we can identify
\pi^{2}_{1}(X)
with the group
{\sf Aut}_{{\sf Cov}_{2}(X)}(p_{U}).
This is convenient, because this definition is independent of the choice of a basepoint. The action of
\pi^{2}_{1}(X)
on
U
induces an action of
\pi^{2}_{1}(X)
on
\Omega_{n}(U).
This defines a structure of
\mathbb{K}[\pi^{2}_{1}(X)]
-module on
\Omega_{n}(U).

Proposition 3.20
.

There is an isomorphism of left
\mathbb{K}[\pi_{1}^{2}(X)]
-modules

(3.31)

\Omega_{n}(U)\cong\mathbb{K}[\pi_{1}^{2}(X)]\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\Omega_{n}(X),

which makes the diagram

(3.32)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{n}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{K}[\pi_{1}^{2}(X)]\otimes\Omega_{n}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{n}(X)}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{n}(p)}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon\otimes{\sf id}}

commutative, where
\varepsilon:\mathbb{K}[\pi_{1}^{2}(X)]\to\mathbb{K}
is the augmentation of the group algebra.

Proof.

It is easy to see that for any vertices
x,x^{\prime}
the module
\Omega_{n}(U,p^{-1}(x),p^{-1}(x^{\prime}))
is a
\mathbb{K}[\pi^{2}_{1}(X)]
-submodule of
\Omega_{n}(U).
Moreover, there is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{K}[\pi_{1}^{2}(X)]
-modules

(3.33)

\Omega_{n}(U)\cong\bigoplus_{x,x^{\prime}}\Omega_{n}(U,p^{-1}(x),p^{-1}(x^{\prime})).

Therefore, it is sufficient to prove the isomorphism

(3.34)

\Omega_{n}(U,p^{-1}(x),p^{-1}(x^{\prime}))\cong\mathbb{K}[\pi^{2}_{1}(X)]\otimes\Omega_{n}(X,x,x^{\prime})

for any vertices
x,x^{\prime}.
Further in the proof we fix a point
x^{\prime},
and consider this arbitrary point as the basepoint
x_{0}:=x^{\prime}
of
X
and chose a basepoint
u_{0}\in p^{-1}(x_{0}).
Hence, it is sufficient to prove the isomorphism

(3.35)

\Omega_{n}(U,p^{-1}(x),p^{-1}(x_{0}))\cong\mathbb{K}[\pi^{2}_{1}(X)]\otimes\Omega_{n}(X,x,x_{0})

(We consider different points as a basepoint to define the isomorphism on different summands. That is why it is convenient for us to identify the group
\pi^{2}_{1}(X)
with the group

{\sf Aut}_{{\sf Cov}_{2}(X)}(U),
which is independent of the choice of the basepoint). Recall that for any
u,u^{\prime}\in U_{0}
we denote by
\alpha_{u,u^{\prime}}:u\to u^{\prime}
the unique morphism in
\Pi^{2}(U).
Then
\Omega_{n}(U,u,u^{\prime})\cong\Omega_{n}(U,\alpha_{u,u^{\prime}}).

We define a map

(3.36)

f:\Omega_{n}(U,p^{-1}(x),p^{-1}(x_{0}))\to\mathbb{K}[\pi^{2}_{1}(X)]\otimes\Omega_{n}(X,x,x_{0})

such that its restriction
f_{u,u^{\prime}}:\Omega_{n}(U,u,u^{\prime})\to\mathbb{K}[\pi^{2}_{1}(X)]\otimes\Omega_{n}(X,x,x_{0})
is defined by the formula

f_{u,u^{\prime}}(\omega)=\theta(u^{\prime})\otimes p(\omega)

(for the definition of
\theta
see (
3.17
)).
By Proposition
3.19

f_{u,u^{\prime}}
induces an isomorphism
\mathbb{K}
-modules

(3.37)

\Omega_{n}(U,u,u^{\prime})\cong(\mathbb{K}\cdot\theta(u^{\prime}))\otimes\Omega_{n}(X,p(\alpha_{u,u^{\prime}})).

If we take the direct sum by all
u\in p^{-1}(x_{0})
and
u^{\prime}\in p^{-1}(x^{\prime}),
we obtain that
f
is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{K}
-modules. Now, in order to prove the isomorphism of
\mathbb{K}[\pi_{1}^{2}(X)]
-modules, we only need to check that
f
is a homomorphism of
\mathbb{K}[\pi_{1}^{2}(X)]
-modules. Take
\gamma\in\pi_{1}^{2}(X).
Since Deck transformations are automorphisms of covers, we obtain
p(\gamma\omega)=p(\omega).
Using this and the definition of the Deck transformation (
3.18
) we get

(3.38)

f(\gamma\omega)=f_{\gamma u,\gamma u^{\prime}}(\gamma\omega)=\theta(\gamma u^{\prime})\otimes p(\omega)=\gamma\theta(u^{\prime})\otimes p(\omega)=\gamma f(\omega).

The fact that the triangle (
3.32
) is commutative is obvious:
(\varepsilon\otimes{\sf id})(f_{u,u^{\prime}}(\omega))=(\varepsilon\otimes{\sf id})(\theta(u^{\prime})\otimes p(\omega))=p(\omega).

∎

Corollary 3.21
.

There is an isomorphism of complexes

(3.39)

\mathbb{K}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}[\pi_{1}^{2}(X)]}\Omega(U)\cong\Omega(X)

defined by the formula
a\otimes\omega\mapsto ap(\omega).

Proof.

Using that
p(\gamma\omega)=p(\omega)
for any
\gamma\in\pi_{1}^{2}(X),
we see that there is a well-defined morphism of complexes
\mathbb{K}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}[\pi_{1}^{2}(X)]}\Omega(U)\to\Omega(X)
defined by
a\otimes\omega\mapsto ap(\omega).
Then we need to check that for any
n
the map
\mathbb{K}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}[\pi_{1}^{2}(X)]}\Omega_{n}(U)\to\Omega_{n}(X)
is an isomorphism. It follows from the commutativity of the diagram (
3.32
) and the isomorphism
\mathbb{K}\otimes_{\mathbb{K}[\pi_{1}^{2}(X)]}\mathbb{K}[\pi_{1}^{2}(X)]\cong\mathbb{K}
.
∎

Remark 3.22
.

Note that we don’t claim that the complexes
\Omega(U)
and
\mathbb{K}[\pi_{1}^{2}(X)]\otimes\Omega(X)
are isomorphic.

Theorem 3.23
.

Let
X
be a connected digraph and

p_{U}:U\to X
be the universal
2
-covering.Then there is a spectral sequence
E
of homological type such that

(3.40)

E^{2}_{i,j}=H_{i}(\pi^{2}_{1}(X),\mathsf{PH}_{j}(U))\Rightarrow\mathsf{PH}_{i+j}(X),

where
\mathsf{PH}_{*}(U)
is treated as a
\pi^{2}_{1}(X)
-module with the action by Deck transformations, and
H_{i}(\pi_{1}^{2}(X),-)
is the group homology of the group
\pi_{1}^{2}(X).

Proof.

We take the complex
\Omega_{n}(U),
the functor of
\pi_{1}^{2}(X)
-coinvarints
H_{0}(\pi_{1}^{2}(X),-):{\sf Mod}(\mathbb{K}[\pi_{1}^{2}(X)])\to{\sf Mod}(\mathbb{K})
and consider two associated hyperhomology spectral sequences
{}^{I}E,{}^{I\!I}E
(
[
20
, Prop.5.7.6]
).

(3.41)

\begin{split}{}^{I}E_{i,j}^{1}=H_{j}(\pi_{1}^{2}(X),\Omega(U)_{j}))&\Rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{i+j}(\pi_{1}^{2}(X),\Omega(U)).\\
{}^{I\!I}E_{i,j}^{2}=H_{i}(\pi_{1}^{2}(X),\mathsf{PH}_{j}(U))&\Rightarrow\mathbb{H}_{i+j}(\pi_{1}^{2}(X),\Omega(U)).\end{split}

If
S
is a set and
\mathbb{K}\cdot S
is a free module generated by
S,
then for any
\mathbb{K}
-module
M
we have isomorphisms
(\mathbb{K}\cdot S)\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}M\cong M^{\oplus S}\cong(\mathbb{Z}\cdot S)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}M.
Therefore,
the isomorphism
\mathbb{K}[\pi_{1}^{2}(X)]\cong\mathbb{K}\cdot\pi_{1}^{2}(X)
and Proposition
3.20
imply

(3.42)

\Omega_{n}(U)\cong\mathbb{K}[\pi_{1}^{2}(X)]\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}\Omega_{n}(X)\cong\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}^{2}(X)]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Omega_{n}(X).

Combining this with
[
3
, Ch.III, Cor.6.6]
we obtain
H_{j}(\pi_{1}^{2}(X),\Omega(U))=0
for
j\neq 0
. Corollary
3.21
implies
H_{0}(\pi_{1}^{2}(X),\Omega(U))=\Omega(X).
Therefore,
{}^{I}E^{1}_{i,j}=0
for
j\neq 0
and

{}^{I}E^{1}_{*,0}=\Omega(X).
It follows that
\mathbb{H}_{*}(\pi_{1}^{2}(X),\Omega(U))=\mathsf{PH}_{*}(X).
Then
{}^{I\!I}E
is the required spectral sequence.
∎

Corollary 3.24
.

Under assumption of Theorem
3.23
, if
\widetilde{\mathsf{PH}}_{*}(U)=0,
then

(3.43)

\mathsf{PH}_{*}(X)\cong H_{*}(\pi_{1}^{2}(X)).

3.10.
Examples

It is easy to check that the morphism of digraphs illustrated on the following picture is a
2
-covering but not a
3
-covering.

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 14.22636pt\

Here
p(x)=x({\rm mod}\ 3).
One can generalize this example considering a cycle of length
n\geq 3.
This will be an example of an
n-1
-covering but not an
n
-covering. In this case it is easy to see that
\widetilde{\mathsf{PH}}_{*}(E)=0,

\pi_{1}^{2}(E)=1
and
\pi_{1}^{2}(X)=\mathbb{Z}.
Therefore Corollary
3.24
implies that
\mathsf{PH}_{*}(X)=H_{*}(\mathbb{Z}).

In the next example we consider a directed triangle instead of the cyclic triangle from the previous example. It easy to check that morphism of digraphs illustrated on the following picture is a 1-covering but not a 2-covering.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip 14.22636pt\

The fact that it is not a 2-covering can be checked by hand but it also obviously follows from the fact that
\pi^{2}_{1}(X)=1
in this case, and hence,
X
has no non-trivial
2
-coverings.

A more complicated example of a universal 2-covering is illustrated on the following picture.

In this case one can also compute that
\widetilde{\mathsf{PH}}_{*}(U)=0,

\pi_{1}^{2}(U)=1
and
\pi_{1}^{2}(X)={\sf Aut}(p_{U})=\mathbb{Z}.
Therefore
\mathsf{PH}_{*}(X)=H_{*}(\mathbb{Z}).

4.
Cayley digraphs

4.1.
1-fundamental group of a Cayley digraph

Let
G
be a group and
S\subseteq G\setminus\{1\}
be a generating subset. Then the Cayley digraph
\mathsf{Cay}(G,S)
is a digraph, whose set of vertices is
G
and the set of arrows consists of couples
(g,gs),
where
g\in G
and
s\in S.
The assumption that
S
generates
G
implies that the digraph is connected. There is also a natural choice of a basepoint:
1\in G.
So
\mathsf{Cay}(G,S)
is a connected pointed digraph.

We denote by
F(S)
the free group generated by
S,
by
\iota:S\to F(S)
the canonical embedding and we set
\bar{s}=\iota(s).
We use these notations in order to distinguish
s\in G
from
\bar{s}\in F(S).
Consider the epimorphism
\sigma:F(S)\twoheadrightarrow G
that sends
\bar{s}
to
s
and set

(4.1)

\mathsf{Rel}(G,S)={\sf Ker}(\sigma:F(S)\twoheadrightarrow G).

We denote by
Q_{S}
the quiver with one vertex and
(Q_{S})_{1}=S.
The free groupoid defined by
Q_{S}
is the free group generated by
S
(which is treated as a groupoid with one object)
F(Q_{S})=F(S).
Consider a morphism of quivers

(4.2)

r:Q(\mathsf{Cay}(G,S))\to Q_{S}

that sends an arrow
(g,gs)
to
s,
for
s\in S.

Proposition 4.1
.

The morphism of free groupoids
F(r):F(\mathsf{Cay}(G,S))\to F(S)
is a covering groupoid corresponding to the subgroup

\mathsf{Rel}(G,S)
of
F(S).
In particular, it induces an isomorphism

(4.3)

r_{*}:\pi_{1}^{1}(\mathsf{Cay}(G,S))\cong\mathsf{Rel}(G,S).

Proof.

It is easy to see that
r
is a covering quiver. Example
3.7
shows that
F(r)
is a covering groupoid. Note that
F(\mathsf{Cay}(G,S))=\Pi^{1}(\mathsf{Cay}(G,S)).
By Proposition
3.10
the map
\pi_{1}^{1}(\mathsf{Cay}(G,S))\to F(S)
is injective. So we only need to prove that the image of
\pi_{1}^{1}(\mathsf{Cay}(G,S))\to F(S)
is
\mathsf{Rel}(G,S).

We claim that for any morphism
\gamma
in
F(Q(\mathsf{Cay}(G,S)))
there is an equation
\sigma(F(r)(\gamma))={\sf dom}(\gamma)^{-1}{\sf cod}(\gamma)
in
G.
Indeed, these two maps
\sigma F(r)
and
{\sf dom}(-)^{-1}{\sf cod}(-)
are both morphisms of groupoids and they coincide on the generating set
(g,gs).
Therefore,
{\sf dom}(\gamma)={\sf cod}(\gamma)
if and only if
F(r)(\gamma)\in\mathsf{Rel}(G,S).

∎

4.2.
l
-fundamental groupoid of a Cayley digraph

We denote by
W^{l}(S)\subseteq F(S)
the set of all elements of the free group
F(S)
that can be presented as
(\bar{s}_{1}\dots\bar{s}_{n})\cdot(\bar{t}_{1}\dots\bar{t}_{m})^{-1},
where
n,m\leq l
and
s_{i},t_{j}\in S
. Consider a normal subgroup
\mathsf{Rel}^{l}(G,S)
of
F(S)
generated by the intersection
\mathsf{Rel}(G,S)\cap W^{l}(S).

(4.4)

\mathsf{Rel}^{l}(G,S)=\langle\mathsf{Rel}(G,S)\cap W^{l}(S)\rangle^{F(S)}.

We also set

(4.5)

\rho^{l}(G,S)=\mathsf{Rel}(G,S)/\mathsf{Rel}^{l}(G,S).

Further we treat
\Pi^{l}(X)
as a quotient of
\Pi^{1}(X)
by relations (
2.11
). In particular,
\pi_{1}^{l}(X)
is a quotient of
\pi_{1}^{1}(X).

Proposition 4.2
.

The isomorphism (
4.3
) induces an isomorphism

(4.6)

\pi^{l}_{1}(\mathsf{Cay}(G,S))\cong\rho^{l}(G,S).

Proof.

For simplicity in the proof we omit
\mathsf{Cay}(G,S)
and
(G,S)
in notations:
\Pi^{1}=\Pi^{1}(\mathsf{Cay}(G,S)),\mathsf{Rel}=\mathsf{Rel}(G,S)
and so on. For a sequence of elements
s_{1},\dots s_{n}\in S
and
g\in G
we consider the morphism

w_{g}(s_{1},\dots s_{n})=(g,gs_{1})\cdot(gs_{1},gs_{1}s_{2})\dots(gs_{1}\dots s_{n-1},gs_{1}\dots s_{n})

in
\Pi^{1}(g,gs_{1}\dots s_{n})
. Denote by
H
the normal subgroup of
\pi_{1}^{1}
generated by elements of the form
w_{1}(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})\cdot w_{1}(t_{1},\dots,t_{m})^{-1},
where
s_{1}\dots s_{n}=t_{1}\dots t_{m}
and
n,m\leq l.
We just need to prove that
H
is the kernel of the map
\pi_{1}^{1}\to\pi_{1}^{l}
and

r_{*}(H)=\mathsf{Rel}^{l}.
The fact that the image of
H
in
\mathsf{Rel}
is
\mathsf{Rel}^{l}
is straightforward: the image of
w_{1}(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})\cdot w_{1}(t_{1},\dots,t_{m})^{-1}
is
(\bar{s}_{1}\dots\bar{s}_{n})\cdot(\bar{t}_{1}\dots\bar{t}_{m})^{-1}\in W^{l}(S).
Prove that
H
is the kernel of
\pi_{1}^{1}\to\pi_{1}^{l}.

By Proposition
2.2
the groupoid
\Pi^{l}
is the quotient of
\Pi^{1}
by the minimal congruence relation such that
w_{g}(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})\sim w_{g}(t_{1},\dots,t_{m})

where
s_{1}\dots s_{n}=t_{1}\dots t_{m},

s_{i},t_{i}\in S,

g\in G
and
n,m\leq l.
Note that the morphism
\Pi^{1}\to\Pi^{l}
is identical on objects, and hence
\Pi^{l}=\Pi^{1}/\mathpzc{H},
where
\mathpzc{H}
is a totally disconnected normal subgroupoid
[
4
, (8.3.2)]
. The totally disconnected normal subgroupoid
\mathpzc{H}
is generated by the elements of the form
w_{g}(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})\cdot w_{g}(t_{1},\dots,t_{m})^{-1}.
Then we need to prove that
H=\mathpzc{H}(1,1).
The group
\mathpzc{H}(1,1)
is the subgroup of
\pi_{1}^{1}
generated by elements of the form

(4.7)

\gamma\cdot w_{g}(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})\cdot w_{g}(t_{1},\dots,t_{m})^{-1}\cdot\gamma^{-1},

where
\gamma\in\Pi^{1}(1,g),

s_{1}\dots s_{n}=t_{1}\dots t_{m}
and
n,m\leq l.
So we just need to prove that an element of the form (
4.7
) is in
H.
Since
r_{*}:\pi_{1}^{1}\to\mathsf{Rel}
is an isomorphism and
r_{*}(H)=\mathsf{Rel}^{l}
, it is enough to check that the image of the element (
4.7
) in
\mathsf{Rel}
is in
\mathsf{Rel}^{l}.
The image is equal to
F(r)(\gamma)\cdot(s_{1}\dots s_{n})\cdot(t_{1}\dots t_{m})^{-1}\cdot F(r)(\gamma)^{-1},
which is conjugated to the element
(s_{1}\dots s_{n})\cdot(t_{1}\dots t_{m})^{-1}\in\mathsf{Rel}^{l}.

∎

4.3.
Universal
l
-covering of a Cayley digraph

For a subset
S\subseteq G
we set

(4.8)

S^{[l]}=\{s_{1}\cdot{\dots}\cdot s_{n}\mid s_{i}\in S,\ 0\leq n\leq l\}.

Note that for
n=0
we obtain
1\in S^{[l]}.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
\varphi:H\to G
be a group epimorphism and let
T\subseteq H\setminus\{1\},

S\subseteq G\setminus\{1\}
be generating subsets such that
\varphi
induces bijections
T\cong S
and
T^{[l]}\cong S^{[l]}.
Then the morphism of digraphs

(4.9)

\mathsf{Cay}(H,T)\to\mathsf{Cay}(G,S)

is an
l
-covering.

Proof.

Since the map
T\to S
is a bijection, it is obvious that the map is a
1
-covering. By Proposition
3.3
, in order to check that it is an
l
-covering, it is sufficient to check that for any paths
(h_{0},\dots,h_{n})
and
(h^{\prime}_{0},\dots,h^{\prime}_{m})
in
\mathsf{Cay}(H,T)
such that
h_{0}=h^{\prime}_{0},\varphi(h_{n})=\varphi(h^{\prime}_{m})
(resp.
\varphi(h_{0})=\varphi(h^{\prime}_{0}),h_{n}=h^{\prime}_{m}
) and
0\leq n,m\leq l
we have
h_{0}=h^{\prime}_{0},h_{n}=h^{\prime}_{m}.
Assume that
h_{0}=h^{\prime}_{0},\varphi(h_{n})=\varphi(h^{\prime}_{m}).
Consider the sequences
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}
and
t_{1}^{\prime},\dots,t^{\prime}_{m}
from
T\cup\{1\}
such that
h_{i}=h_{0}\tilde{t}_{1}\dots\tilde{t}_{i}
and
h^{\prime}_{j}=h_{0}\tilde{t}^{\prime}_{1}\dots\tilde{t}^{\prime}_{j}.
The equation
\varphi(h_{n})=\varphi(h^{\prime}_{m})
implies the equation
\varphi(t_{1}\dots t_{n})=\varphi(t^{\prime}_{1}\dots t^{\prime}_{m})
in
G.
By the assumption, we obtain
t_{1}\dots t_{n}=t^{\prime}_{1}\dots t^{\prime}_{m}.
Therefore
h_{n}=h^{\prime}_{m}.

∎

We say that the generating subset
S\subseteq G
is an
l
-freely generating set
of
G,
if
\mathsf{Rel}(G,S)=\mathsf{Rel}^{l}(G,S).

Proposition
4.2
implies that
S
is
l
-freely generating set if and only if
\pi_{1}^{l}({\sf Cay}(G,S))=1.

Example 4.4
.

(1)

S
is
1
-freely generating if and only if the map
F(S)\to G
is an isomorphism.

(2)

G
is
2
-freely generating set for itself, because
\mathsf{Rel}(G,G)
is generated by elements
\bar{g}\cdot\bar{h}\cdot(\overline{gh})^{-1}\in W^{2}(G).

(3)

If
G=\mathbb{Z}^{n}
then the standard basis
S=\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\}
is a
2
-freely generating set of
G.

(4)

If
G=\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z},
then
S=\{1+n\mathbb{Z}\}
is
n
-freely generating set for
G
but not
(n-1)
-freely generating.

We set

(4.10)

F^{l}(G,S):=F(S)/\mathsf{Rel}^{l}(G,S).

and denote by
\tilde{S}
the image of
S
in
F^{l}(G,S),
and by
\tilde{s}\in\tilde{S}
the image of an element
s\in S.
It is easy to see that
\mathsf{Rel}^{l}(G,S)=\mathsf{Rel}^{l}(F^{l}(G,S),\tilde{S}).
Therefore
\tilde{S}
is
l
-freely generating in
F^{l}(G,S).
The homomorphism
\sigma
induces an epimorphism
\sigma^{l}:F^{l}(G,S)\twoheadrightarrow G,
which induces bijections
\tilde{S}\cong S
and
\tilde{S}^{[l]}\cong S^{[l]}.
Note that
S
is
l
-freely generating if and only if the map
\sigma^{l}:F^{l}(G,S)\to G
is an isomorphism. Moreover
\sigma^{l}
is a universal homomorphism from a group with an
l
-generating subset in the following sense.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
\varphi:H\to G
be a group epimorphism and let
T\subseteq H,

S\subseteq G
be generating subsets such that
\varphi
induces bijections
T\cong S
and
T^{[l]}\cong S^{[l]}.
Assume that
T
is
l
-freely generating. Then there is a unique isomorphism

(4.11)

\theta:F^{l}(G,S)\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}H

such that
\varphi(\tilde{S})=T
and the diagram

(4.12)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{l}(G,S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{l}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

is commutative.

Proof.

If such an isomorphism exists, the commutativity of the diagram implies that
\theta(\tilde{s})=\varphi^{-1}(s).
This proves that, if it exists, it is unique.
Consider a homomorphism
\theta^{\prime}:F(S)\to H
defined by
\theta^{\prime}(\bar{s})=\varphi^{-1}(s).
Let us check that
\mathsf{Rel}^{l}(G,S)\subseteq\mathsf{Ker}(\theta^{\prime}).
Take
(\bar{s}_{1}\dots\bar{s}_{n})\cdot(\bar{s}^{\prime}_{1}\dots\bar{s}^{\prime}_{m})^{-1}\in W^{l}(G,S)\cap\mathsf{Rel}(G,S),
where
n,m\leq l.
Then
\varphi(\theta^{\prime}(\bar{s}_{1})\dots\theta^{\prime}(\bar{s}_{n}))=s_{1}\dots s_{n}=s^{\prime}_{1}\dots s^{\prime}_{m}=\varphi(\theta^{\prime}(\bar{s}^{\prime}_{1})\dots\theta^{\prime}(\bar{s}^{\prime}_{m}))
in
G.
Since
T^{[l]}\to S^{[l]}
is a bijection, we obtain
\theta^{\prime}(\bar{s}_{1})\dots\theta^{\prime}(\bar{s}_{n})=\theta^{\prime}(\bar{s}^{\prime}_{1})\dots\theta^{\prime}(\bar{s}^{\prime}_{m}).
Therefore
(\bar{s}_{1}\dots\bar{s}_{n})\cdot(\bar{s}^{\prime}_{1}\dots\bar{s}^{\prime}_{m})^{-1}\in\mathsf{Ker}(\theta^{\prime}).
Hence,
\theta^{\prime}
induces a homomorphism
\theta:F^{l}(G,S)\to H
such that
\theta(\tilde{s})=\varphi^{-1}(s).
Let us prove that
\theta
is an isomorphism. The homomorphism
\varphi
induces a homomorphism
F^{l}(\varphi):F^{l}(H,T)\to F^{l}(G,S).
Since
T
is
l
-freely generating,
\sigma^{l}:F^{l}(H,T)\to H
is an isomorphism. This defines a homomorphism
F^{l}(\varphi)(\sigma^{l})^{-1}:H\to F^{l}(G,S).
It is easy to check that this homomorphism defines the inverse bijection on generators
T\overset{\cong}{\to}\tilde{S}.
Therefore
\theta
is an isomorphism.
∎

Proposition 4.6
.

The morphism of Cayley digraphs

{\sf Cay}(F^{l}(G,S),\tilde{S})\to{\sf Cay}(G,S)

is the universal
l
-covering.

Proof.

Since
\tilde{S}
is
l
-freely generating in
F^{l}(G,S)
, by Proposition
4.2
the group
\pi_{1}^{l}({\sf Cay}(F^{l}(G,S),\tilde{S}))
is trivial. Therefore, we just need to prove that the map is an
l
-covering. By the definition of
\mathsf{Rel}^{l}(G,S)
it is easy to see that
\tilde{S}^{[l]}\to S^{[l]}
is a bijection. Then the fact that it is an
l
-covering follows from Proposition
4.3
.
∎

4.4.
Path homology of a Cayley digraph

For a group
G
and a generating subset
S\subseteq G
we set

(4.13)

\tilde{G}=F^{2}(G,S).

Theorem 4.7
.

Let
G
be a group and
S
be its generating subset and
\rho:=\rho^{2}(G,S).
Then there is a spectral sequence of homological type

(4.14)

E^{2}_{i,j}=H_{i}(\rho,\mathsf{PH}_{j}(\mathsf{Cay}(\tilde{G},\tilde{S})))\Rightarrow\mathsf{PH}_{i+j}(\mathsf{Cay}(G,S)),

where
H_{*}(\rho,-)
is the group homology of the group
\rho,
and
\mathsf{PH}_{*}(\mathsf{Cay}(\tilde{G},\tilde{S}))
is treated as a
\mathbb{K}[\rho]
-module, where the action of
\rho\subseteq\tilde{G}
is defined by shifts in
\tilde{G}.

Proof.

It follows from Theorem
3.23
and Proposition
4.6
.
∎

We say that a digraph
X
is
\mathbb{K}
-acyclic, if
\tilde{\mathsf{PH}}_{*}(X)=0.

Corollary 4.8
.

Under assumptions of Theorem
4.7
, if
\mathsf{Cay}(\tilde{G},\tilde{S})
is
\mathbb{K}
-acyclic, then

(4.15)

\mathsf{PH}_{*}(\mathsf{Cay}(G,S))\cong H_{*}(\rho).

4.5.
Cayley digraphs of abelian groups

In this subsection we will consider abelian groups with additive notation. We say that a digraph
X
is
\mathbb{K}
-acyclic, if
\tilde{\mathsf{PH}}_{*}(X)=0.

Lemma 4.9
.

The digraph
\mathsf{Cay}(\mathbb{Z},\{1\})
is
\mathbb{K}
-acyclic for any
\mathbb{K}
.

Proof.

The digraph
\mathsf{Cay}(\mathbb{Z},\{1\})
is the union of finite induced line digraphs
I^{-n,n}
on the set
\{-n,-n+1,\dots,n-1,n\}
for
n\geq 1.
Then
\mathsf{Cay}(\mathbb{Z},\{1\})={\sf colim}I^{-n,n}.
By Proposition
1.14
we obtain
\mathsf{PH}_{*}(\mathsf{Cay}(\mathbb{Z},\{1\}))={\sf colim}\>\mathsf{PH}_{*}(I^{-n,n}).
Then the assertion follows from the fact that
I^{-n,n}
is contractible.
∎

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
G
be a free abelian group with a basis
S.
Then
\mathsf{Cay}(G,S)
is
\mathbb{K}
-acyclic for any
\mathbb{K}.

Proof.

If
S
is finite, then it follows from Lemma
4.9
, the formula

(4.16)

\mathsf{Cay}(\mathbb{Z}^{n},\{s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\})=\overset{n}{\underset{i=1}{\Box}}\mathsf{Cay}(\mathbb{Z},\{1\}),

where
s_{1},\dots,s_{n}
is the standard basis, and the Künneth theorem for the box product. If
S
is infinite, we use the fact that
F
is the filtered colimit of finitely generated free subgroups
\langle S^{\prime}\rangle,
where
S^{\prime}\subseteq S
is finite, and use Proposition
1.14
.
∎

Theorem 4.11
.

Let
G
be an abelian group and
S\subseteq G\setminus\{0\}
be a generating subset such that

(1)

for any
s,s^{\prime}\in S
we have
s+s^{\prime}\notin S\cup\{0\};

(2)

for any
s_{1},s_{1}^{\prime},s_{2},s_{2}^{\prime}\in S
the equation
s_{1}+s_{1}^{\prime}=s_{2}+s_{2}^{\prime}
implies that either
(s_{1},s_{1}^{\prime})=(s_{2},s_{2}^{\prime})
or
(s_{1},s_{1}^{\prime})=(s_{2}^{\prime},s_{2}).

Consider the canonical epimorphism from the free abelian group
\mathbb{Z}^{\oplus S}\twoheadrightarrow G
and denote its kernel by

\rho={\sf Ker}(\mathbb{Z}^{\oplus S}\twoheadrightarrow G).

Then there is an isomorphism

(4.17)

\mathsf{PH}_{*}(\mathsf{Cay}(G,S))=\Lambda^{*}(\rho\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{K}),

where
\Lambda^{*}
denotes the exterior algebra over
\mathbb{K}.

Proof.

It is easy to see that the standard basis
\tilde{S}=\{e_{s}\mid s\in S\}
in
\mathbb{Z}^{\oplus S}
is a 2-freely generating subset and the map
\tilde{S}\to S
is a bijection. The assumptions (1),(2) imply that
\tilde{S}^{[2]}\to S^{[2]}
is a bijection. Then by Proposition
4.5
we obtain

\tilde{G}=\mathbb{Z}^{\oplus S}.

Lemma
4.10
implies that
\mathsf{Cay}(\tilde{G},\tilde{S})
is
\mathbb{K}
-acyclic. Then Corollary
4.8
implies that
\mathsf{PH}_{*}(\mathsf{Cay}(G,S))=H_{*}(\rho).
Since
\rho
is a subgroup of a free abelian group,
\rho
is also a free abelian, and hence
H_{*}(\rho)=\Lambda^{*}(\rho\otimes\mathbb{K}).

∎

Corollary 4.12
.

Under the assumption of Theorem
4.11
, the modules
\mathsf{PH}_{n}(\mathsf{Cay}(G,S))
are free over
\mathbb{K}.

On the next proposition we give an example of usage of Theorem
4.11
.

Proposition 4.13
.

There is an isomorphism

\mathsf{PH}_{*}(\mathsf{Cay}(\mathbb{Q},\left\{1/n!\mid n\geq 2\right\}))\cong\Lambda^{*}(\mathbb{K}^{\bigoplus\mathbb{N}}).

Proof.

First we need to check the assumptions of Theorem
4.11
.

(1) Assume the contrary, that
\frac{1}{n!}+\frac{1}{m!}=\frac{1}{k!}
for
n,m,k\geq 2.
Then
n,m>k.
Without loss of generality we can assume that
n\geq m>k\geq 2.
Multiplying by
n!,
we obtain an equation for integer numbers

(4.18)

1+\frac{n!}{m!}=\frac{n!}{k!}.

If
n>m,
then
\frac{n!}{m!}
and
\frac{n!}{k!}
are divisible by
n,
and this contradicts to the equation (
4.18
). If
n=m,
then
\frac{n!}{k!}
is divisible by
n,
which contradicts to the equation
2=\frac{n!}{k!}.

(2) Assume that
\frac{1}{n!}+\frac{1}{m!}=\frac{1}{k!}+\frac{1}{l!}.
Without loss of generality we can assume that
n\geq m,k,l.
Multiplying by
n!
we obtain an equation for integers

(4.19)

1+\frac{n!}{m!}=\frac{n!}{k!}+\frac{n!}{l!}.

If
n>m,l,k,
then
\frac{n!}{m!},\frac{n!}{k!},\frac{n!}{l!}
are divisible by
n,
which contradicts to the equation (
4.19
). Therefore
n\in\{m,k,l\}.
If
n=m>k,l
then
\frac{1}{n!}+\frac{1}{m!}<\frac{1}{k!}+\frac{1}{l!}.
Therefore
n\in\{k,l\}.
If
n=k,
then it is easy to see that
m=l;
and dually, if
n=l
, then
k=m.

We proved the assumption of Theorem
4.11
. Therefore, we just need to prove that
\rho\cong\mathbb{Z}^{\oplus\mathbb{N}}.
By the definition
\rho
is the kernel of the map
\mathbb{Z}^{\oplus\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{Q}
that sends the element of the standard basis
e_{n}
to
\frac{1}{(n+1)!}.
So
\rho
is a subgroup of a free abelian group, and then,
\rho
is also free abelian. So we just need to check that
\rho
is not finitely generated. Indeed
\{(n+2)e_{n+1}-e_{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}
is an infinite linearly independent subset in
\rho,
and thus,
\rho\cong\mathbb{Z}^{\oplus\mathbb{N}}.

∎

Further we give some examples that do not satisfy the assumption of Theorem
4.11
.

Proposition 4.14
.

The digraph
\mathsf{Cay}(\mathbb{Z},\{2^{i}\mid 0\leq i\leq n\})
is
\mathbb{K}
-acyclic for any
\mathbb{K}
and
n\geq 0.

Proof.

Set
X^{n}=\mathsf{Cay}(\mathbb{Z},\{2^{i}\mid 0\leq i\leq n\}).

The proof is by induction on
n.
For
n=0
it is the statement of Lemma
4.9
. Assume that the statement holds for
n,
and prove it for
n+1.
Consider two maps
f,g:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z},
where
f(x)=2x
and
g(x)=\lfloor x/2\rfloor.
It is easy to see that
f
defines a morphism of digraphs
f:X^{n}\to X^{n+1}.
We also claim that
g
defines a morphism of digraphs
g:X^{n+1}\to X^{n}.
Let us check it. There are arrows of four types in
X^{n+1}:

(2x,2x+1),

(2x+1,2x+2),

(2x,2x+2^{i}),

(2x+1,2x+1+2^{i})
where
1\leq i\leq n+1.
If we apply
g,
we obtain the following pairs, which are either arrows or equations:
(x,x),

(x,x+1),

(x,x+2^{i-1}),

(x,x+2^{i-1}).
The composition
gf:X^{n}\to X^{n}
is identical. The composition
fg:X_{n+1}\to X^{n+1}
is defined by the formula

(4.20)

(fg)(x)=\begin{cases}x,&\text{ if }x\text{ is odd}\\
x-1,&\text{ if }x\text{ is even}.\end{cases}

Hence, for any
x\in\mathbb{Z}
the pair
(fg(x),x)
is in
X^{n+1}_{1}.
Therefore,
fg
is homotopic to
{\sf id}.
It follows that
\mathsf{PH}_{*}(X^{n+1})=\mathsf{PH}_{*}(X^{n}).

∎

Proposition 4.15
.

Let
\mathbb{Z}[\frac{1}{2}]
denote the group of rational numbers of the form
m/2^{n}.
Then the digraph
\mathsf{Cay}(\mathbb{Z}[\frac{1}{2}],\{\frac{1}{2^{i}}\mid i\geq 0\})
is
\mathbb{K}
-acyclic for any
\mathbb{K}.

Proof.

It is easy to see that
\mathsf{Cay}(\mathbb{Z}[\frac{1}{2}],\{\frac{1}{2^{i}}\mid i\geq 0\})=\bigcup_{n\geq 0}\mathsf{Cay}(\frac{1}{2^{n}}\mathbb{Z},\{\frac{1}{2^{i}}\mid 0\leq i\leq n\}).
By Proposition
1.15
, it is sufficient to prove that
\mathsf{Cay}(\frac{1}{2^{n}}\mathbb{Z},\{\frac{1}{2^{i}}\mid 0\leq i\leq n\})
is
\mathbb{K}
-acyclic. This follows from the fact that the multiplication on
2^{n}
defines an isomorphism between digraphs
\mathsf{Cay}(\frac{1}{2^{n}}\mathbb{Z},\{\frac{1}{2^{i}}\mid 0\leq i\leq n\})
and
\mathsf{Cay}(\mathbb{Z},\{2^{i}\mid 0\leq i\leq n\}),
and Proposition
4.14
.
∎

Proposition 4.16
.

Consider the circle group

\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid|z|=1\}

and its subgroup
G=\{e^{m\pi/2^{n}}\mid n\geq 1\}.
Then

(4.21)

\mathsf{PH}_{*}(\mathsf{Cay}(G,\{e^{\pi/2^{n}}\mid n\geq 1\}))=H_{*}(S^{1}),

where
H_{*}(S^{1})
is the homology of the circle.

Proof.

Note that
G\cong\mathbb{Z}[\frac{1}{2}]/\mathbb{Z}
and this isomorphism takes
\{e^{\pi/2^{n}}\mid n\geq 1\}
to

S:=\{\frac{1}{2^{n+1}}+\mathbb{Z}\mid n\geq 1\}.
The set
\tilde{S}=\{\frac{1}{2^{n+1}}\mid n\geq 1\}
is
2
-freely generating in
\mathbb{Z}[\frac{1}{2}]
and the maps
\tilde{S}\to S
and
\tilde{S}^{[2]}\to S^{[2]}
are bijections (here it is important that
\frac{1}{2^{n+1}}+\frac{1}{2^{m+1}}\notin\mathbb{Z}
for
n,m\geq 1
because
0\in\tilde{S}^{[2]}
). By Proposition
4.5
we obtain
\tilde{G}=\mathbb{Z}[\frac{1}{2}].
Therefore
\rho=\mathbb{Z}.
Note that
H_{*}(\mathbb{Z})=H_{*}(S^{1}).
Then the assertion follows from
Corollary
4.8
and Proposition
4.15
.
∎
